Fundamentals / Numerical mathematics / Approximation and quadrature

Approximation and quadrature

Reconstruct functions from samples or projections, derive deterministic and Monte Carlo quadrature rules, and test whether their error estimates are credible.

Chapters, problem sheets and the applied study in reading order. For a paper copy, use your browser’s print command. Open the worked solutions you want to include before printing.

Read the subject in depth

Each volume develops its arguments in sequence, with worked problems and links to experiments.

  • Function approximation and numerical integration

    Construct approximations from the information actually available. Derive their error and observe what changes when the function, data or sampling rule violates an assumption.

Advanced study across the subject

Each sequence connects the course to detailed chapters in neighbouring subjects. The complete course PDF includes their derivations, examples and exercises.

  1. Approximating a measured field

    Reconstruct a potential from weighted modes. Compare orthogonality in a rectangle, cylinder and sphere, and determine which coefficients can be recovered from noisy measurements.

  2. Sampling, propagation and unresolved scales

    Apply quadrature and spectral approximation to a real aperture, a decaying field and a radiated-power measurement. Look for an observed convergence rate and explain any plateau.

How the course fits together

  1. Chapters 1–4

    Connect samples locally, choose polynomial nodes, then impose smooth joins to obtain splines. Integrating a reconstructed curve produces a quadrature rule with an error inherited from the approximation.

  2. Chapters 5–8

    Choose nodes to integrate moment conditions exactly or adapt intervals where estimates disagree. Divided differences make incremental interpolation possible, while minimax approximation controls the worst pointwise error.

  3. Chapters 9–12

    Represent functions by global frequencies or orthogonal projections. Endpoint regularity can limit convergence. Random quadrature replaces geometrical spacing with sampling and a statistical error estimate.

Chapter 1 · Piecewise interpolation and error bounds

Construct a continuous profile from sensor values, prove a local interpolation bound and distinguish sampling error from measurement uncertainty.

From first principles

Build a line from two measured endpoints

Suppose values \(y_0,y_1\) are known at \(x_0<x_1\) and no slopes are available. The simplest model reproducing both data is a line of constant slope. At an interior point \(x\), its value can be written with the fraction \(t=(x-x_0)/(x_1-x_0)\) of the panel traversed,

\[I(x)=y_0+(x-x_0)\frac{y_1-y_0}{x_1-x_0}=(1-t)y_0+t y_1 .\]

Its weights are nonnegative on the panel and add to one, so constants and endpoint values are reproduced exactly. When the data come from a twice differentiable function with \(|f''|\le M\), curvature controls departure from the line. The two-node remainder gives

\[|f(x)-I(x)|\le\frac{M}{2}(x-x_0)(x_1-x)\le\frac{M(x_1-x_0)^2}{8}.\]

The last bound occurs at the midpoint. Halving panel width reduces this deterministic bound by a factor of four. Measurement errors in the endpoint values still enter through the interpolation weights and must be budgeted separately.

Derivation and applications

Balance curvature bias against uncertainty in interpolated readings

Consider a panel of length \(h=b-a\), with unknown smooth signal \(f\) and two independent noisy observations \(y_a=f(a)+\epsilon_a,\ y_b=f(b)+\epsilon_b\). Assume each error has zero mean and a known variance, and that \(|f''|\le M\) throughout the panel. For \(t=(x-a)/h\), linear interpolation gives the estimator \(\widehat f=(1-t)y_a+ty_b\). Its expectation follows simply by taking the expected value of each measured endpoint,

\[\operatorname{E}[\widehat f]-f(x)=(1-t)f(a)+tf(b)-f(x),\]
\[|\operatorname{bias}|\le\tfrac12 M h^2t(1-t).\]

Independence eliminates the mixed error term when the noise contribution is squared. Consequently the noise variance depends on the interpolation location even on a flat signal,

\[\operatorname{var}(\widehat f)=(1-t)^2s_a^2+t^2s_b^2 .\]

Expanding the mean squared error about the estimator’s own expectation yields a sum of variance and squared bias. For equal endpoint standard deviations \(s\), the midpoint therefore satisfies

\[\operatorname{E}[(\widehat f-f)^2]\le \frac{s^2}{2}+\left(\frac{Mh^2}{8}\right)^2 .\]

Grid refinement changes the second term, whereas independent repeat measurements at each endpoint reduce the first. Common-mode drift adds a covariance contribution that survives averaging between the two endpoints. In particular, an identical unknown offset passes unchanged through the interpolation because the weights sum to one.

Worked example

Determine which uncertainty dominates a midpoint

At two stations \(0.40\,\mathrm m\) apart the reading errors have \(s=0.030\,\mathrm K\), while \(M=0.50\,\mathrm{K/m^2}\). The maximum midpoint bias is \(0.010\,\mathrm K\) and the noise deviation is \(0.0212\,\mathrm K\). Their root-mean-square budget is \(\sqrt{0.030^2/2+0.010^2}=0.0235\,\mathrm K\). It is already dominated by reading noise.

Second application

Select a spacing from a root-mean-square target

For another instrument let \(s=0.020\), \(M=2.00\), and target midpoint root-mean-square error \(0.030\), in consistent units. The permitted bias magnitude is \(\sqrt{0.030^2-0.020^2/2}=0.0265\). Thus \(h\le\sqrt{8(0.0265)/2.00}=0.325\). If the noise were doubled at fixed error target, the remaining squared bias allowance would shrink to \(0.0001\).

Start from this chapter. For a convex quadratic, the chord lies above the curve. Predict the sign and size change of the midpoint error when each source interval is halved.

OneSimulation laboratory · Measure what interpolation does to a heat integral

Reconstruct from the information available

A line of thermometers supplies values \(y_i\) at increasing coordinates \(x_i\). It does not determine a unique continuous function: any function vanishing at every sensor can be added to a candidate profile. Choosing piecewise straight segments asserts that the unresolved curvature between consecutive readings is small enough for the intended measurement. This assumption may be excellent for predicting a mean temperature and poor for estimating a local heat flux, since differentiation increases the influence of measurement noise.

For \(x\in[x_i,x_{i+1}]\), let \(s=(x-x_i)/h_i\), where \(h_i=x_{i+1}-x_i>0\). The unique affine function matching both readings is \(I_hy=(1-s)y_i+sy_{i+1}\). It follows by writing an unknown intercept and slope, imposing the two endpoint values and eliminating both unknown coefficients. The weights are nonnegative and sum to one. The interpolated value lies between adjacent readings, constants are reproduced, and bounded additive errors of size \(\epsilon\) at the two sensors change the interpolant by at most \(\epsilon\). These properties can fail when extrapolating a considerable distance outside the measured interval.

Why the error is quadratic

Suppose \(f\) is twice continuously differentiable on a panel. Fix an interior point \(t\), and choose a coefficient \(c\) so that \(g(x)=f(x)-I_h f(x)-c(x-x_i)(x-x_{i+1})\) also vanishes at \(t\). It already vanishes at both panel endpoints. Rolle’s theorem applied twice gives a point \(\xi\) where \(g''(\xi)=0\). The interpolant has zero second derivative there, so \(c=f''(\xi)/2\). Evaluate the identity at \(t\) to obtain

\[f(t)-I_h f(t)=\frac{f''(\xi)}{2}(t-x_i)(t-x_{i+1}).\]

The product is nonpositive within the interval and has greatest magnitude \(h_i^2/4\) at its midpoint. Thus the uniform error is at most \(h_i^2\max|f''|/8\). If every panel width is halved, an upper bound based on the same curvature falls by four. When curvature is positive, the segment overestimates the function. Knowledge of the curvature sign supplies information that an absolute error bound would discard.

Turn the bound into a sampling decision

On the unit interval consider a temperature \(T=290+8x^2\) K, with \(x\) given numerically in metres. On the first panel of length 0.25 m the endpoint readings are 290 and 290.5 K. Their average is 290.25 K. The actual midpoint is 290.125 K, an overestimate of 0.125 K. The bound \(16(0.25)^2/8=0.125\) K is attained because the curvature is constant. A second panel of the same width has the same maximum interpolation error even though its temperature change is different.

Now allow an uncertainty of 0.03 K at every source node. A conservative total bound is \(2h^2+0.03\) K for \(h\) in metres. Achieving 0.05 K requires \(h\leq0.10\) m, or at least 11 measurements including both ends. No amount of interpolation onto a denser display grid changes this source requirement. For the slope, two readings with opposite errors can change the estimated derivative by \(2\epsilon/h\), which grows when \(h\) shrinks. Choosing sensor spacing therefore depends on the quantity to be estimated. A spacing suitable for accurate point values may require additional smoothing before slopes inferred from noisy data are useful.

Interpret a finite sensor footprint

Sometimes the observation is already a mean over a finite sensor footprint. For a symmetric footprint of width \(w\) centred at \(x\), the mean of a smooth field is \(f(x)+f''(x)w^2/24+O(w^4)\), by Taylor expansion and symmetry. For the quadratic temperature above this correction is exactly \(16w^2/24\) K when \(w\) is measured in metres. With a 0.10 m footprint it is about 0.0067 K above the point value. Interpreting a cell mean as a point reading therefore adds another positive bias before the chord interpolation even begins. This distinction becomes important when comparing a temperature probe with simulation values stored as cell averages.

Distribute measurements according to the curvature they must resolve

An equally spaced grid spends the same sampling budget in every part of a record. Suppose instead that the trajectory is twice continuously differentiable on each of several known regions, with a certified bound \(M_i\) for \(|f''|\) on region \(i\). Over a panel of width \(h_i\) the largest linear interpolation error is at most \(M_i h_i^2/8\). Thus keeping every local bound below a specified tolerance \(\epsilon\) requires \(h_i\le\sqrt{8\epsilon/M_i}\) whenever \(M_i>0\). A region with four times the curvature bound needs twice as many panels per unit length.

For a fixed total number of panels the reverse question is also helpful. In a region of length \(L_i\) with \(n_i\) equal panels its bound is \(M_iL_i^2/(8n_i^2)\). To minimise the largest such bound over positive continuous allocations, equalise those dominant local bounds. Otherwise moving some resolution from a region with a smaller bound to the largest can improve the maximum. The resulting allocation satisfies \(n_i\propto L_i\sqrt{M_i}\). Counts must then be rounded and their actual bounds checked. Known transitions in curvature should occur at grid nodes if each local bound is to apply to its entire panel.

Place nine panels across a changing acceleration

A displacement history lasts three seconds. In the first two seconds its acceleration magnitude is bounded by 2 m/s², and in the last second by 18 m/s². The relative panel allocations are \(2\sqrt2:\sqrt{18}=2:3\). Nine panels cannot achieve exactly that ratio. Try four panels in the first interval and five in the second. Their widths are 0.5 and 0.2 s, giving error bounds \(2(0.5)^2/8=0.0625\) m and \(18(0.2)^2/8=0.0900\) m. Moving one panel to the late region instead gives bounds \(2(2/3)^2/8=0.1111\) m and \(18(1/6)^2/8=0.0625\) m. The four–five allocation has the smaller worst guarantee.

Nine equally spaced panels have width one third second throughout and include the transition at two seconds. Their late bound is 0.25 m, almost three times the nonuniform worst bound. This comparison is about a guaranteed maximum between observations, not only a discrepancy at selected test locations. Piecewise quadratic motions with constant accelerations attain the midpoint bound on each panel; slopes and positions can be matched at the transition without altering either local error.

The reasoning assumes acceleration bounds independent of the sampled readings. Estimating them from those same coarse readings can miss a short acceleration pulse entirely. If endpoint measurements also contain error bounded by 0.03 m, the convex interpolation weights add at most 0.03 m to each panel bound. The chosen allocation then certifies 0.12 m. Refining the grid further can reduce curvature error but cannot eliminate the measurement floor without better data.

If the allowable total error were 0.10 m, leaving 0.03 m for sensor uncertainty would allow at most 0.07 m for curvature. Four early panels already meet that condition. The late section would require at least six panels because \(18/(8\cdot6^2)=0.0625\), giving a ten-panel acquisition plan.

Where does a trustworthy interpolant cease to be accurate?

Interpolate the same smooth function on one interval with two and then three nodes. For the line the error changes with curvature, while for the quadratic the node product changes sign at the middle point. Inspect the signed error between observations.

Approximating a resonance with a rational function · Function approximation and numerical integration

A smooth response on a measured interval can be governed by a nearby pole. Derive a low-order Padé fit, construct a barycentric rational approximant from samples and check whether a predicted pole survives independent data.

Chapter 2 · Polynomial interpolation and node placement

Derive the Lagrange interpolant, diagnose amplification of sample noise and use cosine-distributed nodes to control a polynomial error factor.

From first principles

Construct the unique interpolating polynomial

A polynomial of degree at most \(n\) has \(n+1\) coefficients. To interpolate \(n+1\) distinct abscissae \(x_i\), build one basis polynomial for each measurement. The factors \(x-x_j\) make it vanish at every other node; dividing by its value at \(x_i\) gives unit height there,

\[\ell_i(x)=\prod_{j\ne i}\frac{x-x_j}{x_i-x_j},\]
\[p(x)=\sum_{i=0}^n y_i\ell_i(x).\]

At \(x=x_i\) all terms except \(y_i\) disappear. Uniqueness follows because the difference of two such fits would have degree at most \(n\) and at least \(n+1\) distinct roots. The same representation also exposes sensitivity to equal bounded reading errors,

\[|\delta p(x)|\le \left(\sum_{i=0}^n|\ell_i(x)|\right)\max_i|\delta y_i|.\]

The sum depends on the node placement and the evaluation location. Distinctness ensures existence and uniqueness but gives no guarantee of uniformly small amplification at high degree.

Derivation and applications

Derive barycentric weights from the node polynomial

Every cardinal basis polynomial contains nearly the same product of factors. Factoring out the common part leads to a practical evaluation formula. Assume \(n+1\) distinct real nodes and denote their monic node polynomial by \(L(x)=\prod_{i=0}^n(x-x_i)\). At a root \(x_i\) only one term in the derivative survives. Define a weight using that derivative,

\[L'(x_i)=\prod_{j\ne i}(x_i-x_j),\]
\[w_i=\frac1{L'(x_i)},\]
\[\ell_i(x)=L(x)\frac{w_i}{x-x_i}.\]

For \(x\) away from the nodes, express the interpolant as \(L(x)\) times a rational sum. Apply the same formula to a polynomial identically equal to one, using the fact that interpolation reproduces constants. The two identities have the same factor \(L(x)\),

\[p(x)=L(x)\sum_i\frac{w_i y_i}{x-x_i},\]
\[1=L(x)\sum_i\frac{w_i}{x-x_i}.\]

Their ratio cancels the product and gives the barycentric form

\[p(x)=\frac{\sum_i w_i y_i/(x-x_i)}{\sum_i w_i/(x-x_i)} .\]

A common factor in the weights also cancels, allowing rescaling. At a node the individual terms have poles but the limit equals its stored datum; an implementation should return that datum directly on an exact match. The representation improves evaluation without repairing intrinsically poor node placement. If nodes are fixed and many observation sets arrive, their weights can be reused.

Worked example

Reconstruct a calibration at a new input

Use nodes \(0,1,3\), data \(2,1,5\), and common-scaled weights \((2,-3,1)\). At \(x=2\), the three terms in the denominator are \(1,-3,-1\); their data-weighted sum is \(2-3-5=-6\). Thus \(p(2)=(-6)/(-3)=2\). Direct construction gives \(p(x)=x^2-2x+2\), confirming the reconstructed value.

Second application

Derive reusable weights for a different measurement grid

For nodes \(-1,0,1,2\), the four products \(L'(x_i)\) are \(-6,2,-2,6\). Multiplying all reciprocals by six gives weights \((-1,3,-3,1)\). At \(x=1/2\), the normalized interpolation weights reduce to \((-1,9,9,-1)/16\). An isolated unit error at the right endpoint changes this midpoint prediction by \(-1/16\), while a uniform shift of all readings by one moves the result by exactly one.

One polynomial through all measurements

Given \(n+1\) distinct nodes \(x_j\), seek one polynomial \(p\) of degree at most \(n\) matching \(y_j\) at each node. Define \(\ell_j(x)=\prod_{i\ne j}(x-x_i)/(x_j-x_i)\). This basis has value one at its own node and zero at all the others, so \(p=\sum_j y_j\ell_j\) satisfies the observations. If another such polynomial existed, the difference would have \(n+1\) separate zeros despite degree at most \(n\). It must vanish identically. Uniqueness concerns exact arithmetic and says little by itself about sensitivity.

For the nodes \(-1,0,1\), the three basis functions are \(x(x-1)/2,\ 1-x^2,\ x(x+1)/2\). At \(x=1/2\) their values are \(-1/8,3/4,3/8\). They sum to one but are not all positive. Consider samples \(2,1,2\): the predicted value is \(1.25\), consistent with the quadratic \(1+x^2\). If the three readings instead carry independent signed perturbations bounded by \(\epsilon\), the possible prediction error at this point reaches \((1/8+3/4+3/8)\epsilon=1.25\epsilon\). The worst perturbation takes signs matching those of the basis values.

Separate noise and approximation

The maximum of \(\sum_j|\ell_j(x)|\) over the interval, the Lebesgue constant, bounds the operator’s amplification of data errors. A different quantity controls the error of interpolation from exact samples. For sufficiently smooth \(f\), repeating the Rolle argument with \(n+2\) zeros gives \(f(x)-p(x)=f^{(n+1)}(\xi)\prod_j(x-x_j)/(n+1)!\). This formula compares the exact function with the selected polynomial. It contains both a geometric factor controlled by the nodes and a higher derivative that may grow quickly with degree. A small geometric factor cannot by itself establish convergence for an arbitrary function.

On \([-1,1]\) let \(T_m(x)=\cos(m\arccos x)\). From the cosine addition formula, \(T_{m+1}=2xT_m-T_{m-1}\) with \(T_0=1,T_1=x\). Thus \(T_m\) is a polynomial of degree \(m\) with leading coefficient \(2^{m-1}\) for \(m\geq1\). Its \(m\) zeros lie at \(\cos[(2k+1)\pi/(2m)]\). Choosing them as interpolation nodes makes the monic geometric factor \(2^{1-m}T_m(x)\), whose maximum magnitude is exactly \(2^{1-m}\).

Why the alternating extrema matter

At the \(m+1\) points \(\cos(k\pi/m)\), this monic factor alternates between its positive and negative extreme. Suppose another monic degree-\(m\) polynomial had a strictly smaller maximum magnitude. Subtract it from the Chebyshev factor. The leading powers cancel, leaving degree at most \(m-1\). At the successive extrema the difference must alternate in sign, forcing at least \(m\) distinct zeros between them. That contradicts its degree. This minimax argument concerns the node polynomial, and explains the deliberate concentration of nodes near the endpoints.

For \(m=3\) the recommended points are \(0,\pm\sqrt3/2\); their monic factor is \(x^3-3x/4\) with magnitude at most 0.25. The equally spaced alternative \(-1,0,1\) has factor \(x^3-x\), whose largest magnitude is \(2/(3\sqrt3)=0.385\). If data already exist at fixed locations, moving them is seldom an option. Subdividing the interval and fitting lower-degree polynomials locally instead limits long-range coupling. For evaluation at many points, barycentric interpolation can reuse stored node weights while avoiding explicit construction of a large monomial coefficient vector.

Predict the degree from the nearest complex singularity

Well-chosen nodes control amplification of data errors. The rate at which a smooth target itself can be approximated also depends on what happens away from the real interval. Write \(x=(z+z^{-1})/2\). The unit circle maps to \([-1,1]\), and the circle \(|z|=\rho>1\) maps to an ellipse with foci at the endpoints. The Chebyshev polynomial obeys \(T_k(x)=(z^k+z^{-k})/2\). Consequently the Laurent coefficients of \(f((z+z^{-1})/2)\) determine the Chebyshev coefficients of \(f\). If the function extends analytically through the closed ellipse and has magnitude at most \(M\) there, the Cauchy coefficient bound gives \(|a_k|\le2M\rho^{-k}\) for \(k\ge1\). Summing this geometric tail bounds the error of the degree-\(n\) truncated series by \(2M\rho^{-n}/(\rho-1)\) on the interval. A real-axis corner invalidates this analytic argument.

A rational response with an exactly measurable tail

For \(0<q<1\), take \(f(x)=(1-q^2)/(1-2qx+q^2)\). With \(x=\cos\theta\), summing the two geometric series in \(qe^{\pm i\theta}\) gives \(f(x)=1+2\sum_{k\ge1}q^kT_k(x)\). Its pole at \(x=(q+q^{-1})/2\) lies on the ellipse with parameter \(1/q\). The exact coefficients decay like \(q^k\). At \(x=1\) all omitted terms have the same positive sign. Since \(|T_k|\le1\), the uniform truncation error equals \(2q^{n+1}/(1-q)\).

For \(q=1/3\) the pole is at \(5/3\), and the error equals \(3^{-n}\). Degree six leaves error \(1/729\approx0.00137\). Degree seven reduces it to \(1/2187\approx0.000457\). This establishes a degree for a specified error before constructing a node grid. These exact errors concern the truncated series. An interpolant fitted on Chebyshev nodes combines unresolved modes into lower coefficients and need not have precisely the same error, although analyticity still gives geometric convergence.

Practise on sheet 1 · How close can a pole move before the degree budget fails?. Further reading · University of Oxford · Chebyshev Series, Nick Trefethen · Lecture 3, Chebyshev series, part 2, Smoothness and convergence theorems.

Evaluate without forming monomial coefficients

Define barycentric weights \(w_j=1/\prod_{i\ne j}(x_j-x_i)\). Dividing the Lagrange interpolation formula and the identity \(\sum\ell_j=1\) by the common nodal polynomial gives the evaluation ratio \(\sum_j w_j y_j/(x-x_j)\) divided by \(\sum_j w_j/(x-x_j)\). At an exact interpolation node, return its stored value directly. Multiplying all weights by the same constant has no effect, which is useful for numerical scaling.

For nodes \(-1,0,1\) the weights are proportional to \(1,-2,1\). At \(x=1/2\) with data \(2,1,2\), the weighted numerator is \(4/3-4-4=-20/3\), and the denominator is \(2/3-4-2=-16/3\). Their ratio gives 1.25. This evaluates the very same polynomial as the basis construction. Better arithmetic for evaluation cannot remove an intrinsically large Lebesgue constant associated with a poor set of nodes.

Determine exactly how node errors can reinforce

For fixed distinct interpolation nodes let \(L_j(x)\) denote the cardinal polynomials. Changing the readings by errors \(e_j\) changes a reconstructed value by \(\sum_j e_j L_j(x)\). With independent bounds \(|e_j|\le\delta\), taking absolute values gives an amplification no larger than \(\delta\sum_j|L_j(x)|\). At any fixed evaluation point this bound can be attained by choosing the sign of \(e_j\) to agree with the sign of \(L_j(x)\). The sum of absolute cardinal values is therefore the exact pointwise error amplification for this particular error model.

If all reading errors are a single unknown constant instead, the amplification is exactly one because the cardinal polynomials sum to one. The same nodes have different noise behaviour for coherent offsets and independent bounded perturbations. Statistical independence with equal variances provides a third measure: the output variance is \(\sigma^2\sum_jL_j(x)^2\). The worst pointwise absolute sum, its maximum across the interval, and this root-sum-of-squares factor should not be interchanged. They correspond to questions about adversarial signs, location and probabilistic noise, respectively.

Check a three-station calibration inside and beyond its range

Take stations at \(-1,0,1\). Their cardinal polynomials are \(x(x-1)/2,\ 1-x^2,\ x(x+1)/2\). At \(x=1/2\) they take values \((-1/8,3/4,3/8)\). The weights sum to one, but their absolute values sum to \(5/4\). If each observation is accurate to within 0.02 units, the interpolated calibration can be wrong by 0.025 from observations alone. The specific error pattern \((-0.02,0.02,0.02)\) attains that value. Polynomial exactness for noise-free quadratic inputs provides no protection against that sign pattern.

With independent standard deviations 0.02, the same evaluation instead has standard deviation \(0.02\sqrt{1/64+9/16+9/64}=0.02\sqrt{46}/8\approx0.01696\). The smaller value follows from the assumed random cancellation and is not a guaranteed bound. At \(x=2\), beyond the calibration interval, the same weights become \(1,-3,3\). The worst bounded measurement error is now \(7(0.02)=0.14\). Every tabulated station can pass a calibration check while extrapolation is seven times more sensitive than an original reading.

Inside \([0,1]\) the absolute sum simplifies to \(1+x-x^2\); its maximum occurs at one half and equals \(5/4\). Symmetry gives the same maximum on the other half. For quadratic interpolation this gives a complete guarantee throughout the stated interval, with construction of the worst perturbation. A graph of the fitted polynomial itself would reveal none of these conditioning facts. A sensor specification therefore needs both an admissible evaluation range and a description of how the raw station errors may vary together.

The maximum amplification also gives a way to compare retrospective calibrations. If a process must meet a total error of 0.03 units and each raw persistent bounded error may be as large as 0.02, the three-point design leaves at most \(0.03-1.25(0.02)=0.005\) units for polynomial approximation bias throughout the interval. A check at the three stations themselves would assign a larger remaining allowance and would miss the interior maximum.

Integrating cosine interpolants on nested grids · Function approximation and numerical integration

Chebyshev points turn polynomial interpolation into a cosine series. Derive the exact moments of each basis polynomial, explain aliasing and find a case where two consecutive quadrature grids confidently agree on the wrong answer.

Problem sheet 1 · Nodes and interpolation

Analyse local interpolation errors, demonstrate noise amplification under extrapolation and compare two sets of global interpolation nodes.

Estimate a probe requirement

A smooth pressure profile on a 2 m pipe satisfies \(|p''|\leq24\) kPa/m². Each probe has absolute uncertainty 0.01 kPa. Bound the total error of piecewise linear interpolation. Find the largest equal spacing consistent with 0.04 kPa total error and the smallest number of probes including the two endpoints.

Worked solution

Between measured nodes the interpolation error of exact samples is bounded by \(24h^2/8=3h^2\) kPa. The two sample errors enter with nonnegative weights summing to one, adding at most 0.01 kPa. The requirement \(3h^2+0.01\leq0.04\) gives \(h\leq0.10\) m. Twenty intervals on a 2 m pipe require 21 probes.

This calculation controls worst-case pressure values and assumes the curvature estimate holds on every panel. A single unnoticed discontinuity would invalidate its use on that panel.

Predict beyond the sensors

Two sensors at positions 0 and 1 m each have error at most 0.5 K. Compute the interpolation weights at 0.4 m and the extrapolation weights at 1.5 m. Find the largest change to each predicted temperature and provide a sign pattern attaining each bound.

Worked solution

At 0.4 the weights are 0.6 and 0.4. Equal positive errors produce the worst-case rise of 0.5 K. At 1.5 the weights are \(-0.5\) and \(1.5\). Taking the first error negative and the second positive yields \(0.25+0.75=1.0\) K.

Even for a perfectly affine true profile the second estimate is more sensitive to data noise. If the true profile is curved, extrapolation adds an entirely separate approximation error.

Construct a minimax nodal factor

Choose two symmetric nodes \(\pm c\) on \([-1,1]\). Find the maximum magnitude of their monic nodal polynomial and minimise it over \(0\leq c\leq1\). Use the result to bound the interpolation error when \(|f''|\leq4\).

Worked solution

The nodal polynomial is \(x^2-c^2\); its negative extreme is \(-c^2\) at zero and positive extremes occur at the endpoints with value \(1-c^2\). Thus its norm is \(\max(c^2,1-c^2)\), minimised at \(c^2=1/2\). The nodes are \(\pm1/\sqrt2\) and the best monic norm is \(1/2\).

The Lagrange remainder yields maximum error at most \(4(1/2)/2=1\). For endpoints as nodes the same reasoning gives a bound of 2. The change improves this worst-case bound even though the endpoint readings themselves are no longer sampled.

How close can a pole move before the degree budget fails?

For the same rational family, a changed physical parameter gives \(q=1/2\). Locate the pole and find the smallest truncation degree giving uniform error at most 0.002. Compare with \(q=1/3\) at that tolerance. Explain why increasing the density of a plot cannot establish a smaller worst-case error than the derived tail.

Worked solution

The pole is \((1/2+2)/2=1.25\), closer to the interval than \(5/3\). The exact tail is \(2^{1-n}\). At degree nine it is \(1/256\approx0.00391\). At ten it is \(1/512\approx0.00195\). Thus ten is the first sufficient degree.

For \(q=1/3\), degrees five and six give \(1/243\) and \(1/729\), so six suffices. The four extra degrees are explained by the slower coefficient decay. The endpoint \(x=1\) attains the tail exactly, regardless of how densely interior points are plotted.

Revisit chapter 2 · Predict the degree from the nearest complex singularity.

Chapter 3 · Hermite pieces and cubic splines

Build a cubic from endpoint values and slopes, then determine missing curvature information by joining adjacent pieces smoothly.

From first principles

Determine a cubic from endpoint values and slopes

On one panel \([a,b]\), approximate a smooth curve with a cubic and use both endpoint heights and slopes. Let \(h=b-a>0\), \(t=(x-a)/h\), endpoint heights \(y_0,y_1\), and derivatives \(d_0,d_1\) with respect to \(x\). The conditions at the left endpoint immediately fix two coefficients,

\[p(t)=y_0+hd_0t+At^2+Bt^3 .\]

The factor \(h\) converts a derivative per unit \(x\) into one per unit \(t\). Write \(\Delta=y_1-y_0\). Imposing the right endpoint value and derivative gives two linear equations,

\[A+B=\Delta-hd_0,\]
\[2A+3B=h(d_1-d_0).\]

Solving gives \(A=3\Delta-h(2d_0+d_1)\) and \(B=-2\Delta+h(d_0+d_1)\). Thus four measurements determine four coefficients uniquely. Adjacent panels sharing heights and slopes join with a continuous first derivative. If only heights are measured, a spline construction supplies the missing information through smoothness conditions between panels and boundary conditions at the ends.

Derivation and applications

Check whether a cubic panel preserves a rising signal

A cubic matching two heights and slopes can overshoot even when both endpoint heights increase. Consider a Hermite panel with \(y_1>y_0\), length \(h\), slope of the chord \(m=(y_1-y_0)/h\), and endpoint derivatives \(d_0,d_1\). The derivative of the cubic is a quadratic. Express that derivative using the nonnegative Bernstein basis on \(0\le t\le1\),

\[p'(x)=d_0(1-t)^2+2b\,t(1-t)+d_1t^2 .\]

Here \(p'\) is with respect to the physical coordinate \(x\). The first and last coefficients enforce the endpoint derivatives. Each of the three basis functions has integral \(1/3\) over \(t\). Since integrating \(p'(x)\) across the physical panel must return the specified height gain, the middle coefficient is fixed,

\[y_1-y_0=\frac{h}{3}(d_0+b+d_1),\]
\[b=3m-d_0-d_1 .\]

Nonnegative coefficients provide a simple sufficient condition for a nondecreasing panel. The basis functions also sum to one, so they immediately bound the derivative when the condition holds,

\[d_0,d_1\ge0,\]
\[d_0+d_1\le3m\quad\Longrightarrow\quad 0\le p'(x)\le\max(d_0,b,d_1).\]

A negative middle coefficient alone does not prove reversal, since the three basis functions overlap. If the sufficient test fails, evaluate the minimum of the quadratic on the panel, including its endpoints and any interior stationary point. Local monotonicity matters when interpolating cumulative counts or instrument calibrations that must be invertible.

Worked example

Certify a rising calibration between samples

A signal rises from 2 to 5 over \(h=2\), so \(m=1.5\). Reported slopes are \(d_0=1\) and \(d_1=3\), giving \(b=0.5\). The derivative is nonnegative everywhere. At the midpoint it equals \(0.25(1)+0.5(0.5)+0.25(3)=1.25\). No hidden local maximum can lie inside this panel.

Second application

Find a reversal that endpoint slopes conceal

On a separate unit panel take a rise of one and equal endpoint slopes of four. Then \(m=1\), \(b=3-8=-5\), and the midpoint derivative is \(1-2.5+1=-0.5\). Both measured endpoint derivatives are positive, yet the fitted curve locally decreases. Replacing both slopes by \(1.5\) gives \(b=0\) and restores a certifiably nondecreasing interpolation while preserving the two measured heights.

Four conditions on a single panel

Line segments reproduce values but change slope abruptly at a sensor. A trajectory planner may require a smooth velocity as well as an accurate position. On a panel of width \(h\) let \(s=(x-x_i)/h\). A cubic polynomial has four coefficients. Specifying \(y_i,y_{i+1}\) and slopes \(d_i,d_{i+1}\) determines it uniquely. A convenient basis writes the interpolant as

\[H=(2s^3-3s^2+1)y_i+(-2s^3+3s^2)y_{i+1}+h(s^3-2s^2+s)d_i+h(s^3-s^2)d_{i+1}.\]

To reconstruct these terms, seek a cubic multiplying \(y_i\) with value one at \(s=0\), zero at \(s=1\), and zero slope at both ends. The four linear conditions give \(2s^3-3s^2+1\). The other basis functions follow by imposing a unit on their own datum and zero on the remaining three. The factors of \(h\) convert derivatives with respect to physical \(x\) into derivatives with respect to dimensionless \(s\). Dropping these factors would make the interpolant change shape when switching between metres and centimetres.

For positions \(y_0=0,y_1=1\) m over one second with both endpoint velocities zero, \(H=3s^2-2s^3\) m. At half time the position is 0.5 m but the speed is 1.5 m/s, greater than the mean speed of 1 m/s. Imposing rest at both ends forces the intervening speed to exceed the mean somewhere. The piece alone leaves endpoint accelerations \(+6\) and \(-6\) m/s². A smooth join to a subsequent segment therefore needs more than identical endpoint positions.

Recover slopes when only values are measured

A cubic spline fits one cubic on every interval, requiring continuity of both first and second derivatives at interior nodes. Let \(M_i=S''(x_i)\). Since a cubic has a linear second derivative, on panel \(i\) interpolate \(M_i\) and \(M_{i+1}\) linearly and integrate twice. Use the two measured endpoint values to fix the integration constants. On equal panels the resulting right-hand slope at the left endpoint is \((y_{i+1}-y_i)/h-h(2M_i+M_{i+1})/6\). The left-hand slope at the right endpoint is \((y_{i+1}-y_i)/h+h(M_i+2M_{i+1})/6\).

Equating these expressions at an interior join gives \(M_{i-1}+4M_i+M_{i+1}=6(y_{i-1}-2y_i+y_{i+1})/h^2\). This tridiagonal system relates each undetermined curvature to the neighbouring data. Two additional end conditions are needed. A natural spline imposes zero curvature at both endpoints. A clamped spline uses given endpoint slopes instead. Choose those conditions from what the experiment actually controls.

Examine the influence of endpoint assumptions

Fit a natural spline at \(x=0,1,2\) to \(y=0,1,0\). The end curvatures are zero and the interior equation gives \(4M_1=6(0-2+0)=-12\), hence \(M_1=-3\). On the first panel \(S''=-3x\). Two integrations give \(S=-x^3/2+ax+b\). The endpoint values set \(b=0,a=1.5\), yielding \(S(0.5)=0.6875\) compared with 0.5 from the straight chord. Symmetry gives zero slope at the summit and a reflected cubic on the second interval.

Smoothness alone cannot establish the true midpoint value. A sensor measuring a sharp corner would support a piecewise linear description, and monotone data can require a slope limiter to avoid artificial extrema in a cubic fit. Likewise, setting endpoint curvature to zero in a mechanical fit asserts a moment condition only under the appropriate beam model. Report the end conditions with the fitted curve, especially when derivatives near the ends are used as physical observables.

Use independent endpoint information as a check

Suppose the three displacement values in the example come with an independent measurement of the initial slope, \(S'(0)=1\) rather than 1.5. Imposing a natural left endpoint as well as this slope would overdetermine that end. One reasonable choice is a mixed spline, clamped on the left and natural on the right. For \(h=1\), the slope formula at the left reads \(1-(2M_0+M_1)/6=1\), giving \(2M_0+M_1=0\). The interior equation gives \(M_0+4M_1=-12\), since \(M_2=0\).

Solving yields \(M_1=-24/7\) and \(M_0=12/7\). A measured slope at one end has changed curvatures inside the interval. That coupling across segments distinguishes a global spline fit from interpolating each segment independently with prescribed slopes at every node.

Why the natural spline minimises bending

Consider all twice continuously differentiable interpolants of the same tabulated values on an interval. For a natural cubic spline \(S\), each panel is cubic, \(S'\) and \(S''\) agree across knots, and \(S''=0\) at both endpoints. Let a competing interpolant be \(S+w\). The perturbation \(w\) vanishes at every knot including the endpoints. Expanding the squared curvature integral gives \(\int(S''+w'')^2=\int(S'')^2+2\int S''w''+\int(w'')^2\). To prove that \(S\) minimises this integral, account for every boundary term in the cross product.

Integrate the cross product by parts twice on each panel. The interior integral containing \(S''''w\) vanishes because the panel is cubic. The term \(S'''w\) vanishes at every panel endpoint since \(w=0\) at each knot. The remaining interface contributions \(S''w'\) cancel between adjacent panels because both factors are continuous. At the outer boundaries \(S''=0\). Thus the cross term vanishes exactly. Equality of the objectives requires \(w''=0\) throughout; a globally affine perturbation vanishing at two distinct knots is zero. This proves uniqueness within the admissible class and explains the natural endpoint condition as part of a variational statement.

Calculate the bending energy on an uneven grid

Suppose the interpolation points are \((0,0),(1,2),(3,2)\). The panel widths are one and two, and the secant slopes are two and zero. For a natural cubic write \(M_i=S''(x_i)\), so \(M_0=M_2=0\). Continuity of first derivative at the central knot gives \(2(1+2)M_1=6(0-2)\), hence \(M_1=-2\). The bending moment changes linearly from zero to −2 on the first panel and from −2 to zero on the second. The longer panel contains twice the squared curvature integral because it sweeps the same range of curvature over twice the distance.

Specifically the first contribution is \(\int_0^1(-2x)^2dx=4/3\). On the second panel \(S''=x-3\), giving \(\int_1^3(x-3)^2dx=8/3\). Total energy is four. Integrating the first curvature twice and fitting its endpoints gives \(S=7x/3-x^3/3\); at the central knot the slope is \(4/3\), neither of the adjacent straight-line secant slopes. On the second panel write \(t=x-1\). Then \(S=2+4t/3-t^2+t^3/6\), which returns to two at \(t=2\). The curve rises above the equal second and third data values in between.

Minimum bending therefore does not guarantee monotonicity between nearly flat measurements or an absence of overshoot. If flat endpoints describe a known plateau rather than merely two equal observations, a shape constraint changes the admissible functions. The preceding proof still explains why the natural interpolant won its own curvature competition, but gives no reason to prefer it after physical constraints have changed.

For these data the overshoot can even be located analytically. On the second panel the derivative vanishes when \(4/3-2t+t^2/2=0\), giving the interior root \(t=2-2/\sqrt3\), about 0.845. A measured value taken near \(x=1.845\) would therefore help discriminate the minimum-bending curve from a physical plateau. Taking another measurement at either already constrained endpoint would do less to resolve that ambiguity.

Can an interpolated cumulative reading decrease?

A monotonically increasing quantity is sampled at integer times 0 through 4 with values 0, 0.04, 0.08, 1, 1. A natural spline enforces continuous second derivative and zero end curvature. A shape-preserving cubic constrains the node slopes.

Chapter 4 · Composite quadrature and cancellation

Derive midpoint, trapezoidal and Simpson formulas, and use the sign of curvature to interpret a pair of numerical integral estimates.

From first principles

Integrate the line joining two function values

To estimate accumulated area from sampled values, specify how the function is approximated between samples. On one panel of width \(h\), connect \(f(a)\) and \(f(a+h)\) by a straight line. Its integral is its mean height times its width,

\[T_{\rm panel}=\int_0^h\left[(1-s/h)f(a)+(s/h)f(a+h)\right]ds=\frac h2[f(a)+f(a+h)].\]

Summing panels on a uniform mesh counts every interior value twice and each boundary value once. If \(|f''|\le M\), the linear interpolation remainder at distance \(s\) from the left edge is bounded by \(Ms(h-s)/2\). Integrating that bound and adding all panels gives

\[|I-T_h|\le\frac{(b-a)Mh^2}{12}.\]

Halving the step therefore quarters the upper bound for a fixed interval. The actual error can be smaller if curvature changes sign and panel errors cancel. Endpoint singularities or jumps can violate the smoothness assumption and require separate treatment.

Derivation and applications

Explain why periodic trapezoidal quadrature can be unusually accurate

For a periodic smooth signal, the nominal second-order trapezoidal bound can substantially underestimate the observed accuracy. Consider one full period \([0,2\pi]\) and \(N\) equally spaced samples \(x_j=2\pi j/N\). Since the first and final endpoints agree, their half weights combine. For one complex Fourier mode indexed by integer \(k\), the sample mean is a geometric series,

\[\frac1N\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}e^{ikx_j}=\begin{cases}1,&k\text{ is a multiple of }N,\\0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}\]

For the second case the ratio is a nontrivial \(N\)th root of unity; the numerator of the finite sum vanishes. The exact period integral of a nonconstant Fourier mode also vanishes. Expand a signal with absolutely summable Fourier coefficients so summation and sampling can be interchanged. The grid integrates exactly those nonconstant modes which do not alias into the constant mode,

\[f(x)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb Z}c_k e^{ikx},\]
\[T_N=2\pi\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb Z}c_{\ell N},\]
\[I=2\pi c_0 .\]

The full quadrature error is therefore determined by the sampled multiples of \(N\). For a signal with \(|c_k|\le Cq^{|k|}\), \(0<q<1\), summing the two geometric tails gives

\[|T_N-I|\le4\pi C\frac{q^N}{1-q^N}.\]

The conclusion depends on covering a complete period with the matched endpoints. A smooth nonperiodic signal generally acquires a discontinuity or derivative mismatch under periodic extension, changing the coefficient decay.

Worked example

Detect the only troublesome harmonic

Integrate \(f(x)=2+\cos(3x)+0.1\cos(8x)\) with eight equally spaced points around a full period. The true integral is \(4\pi\). The third harmonic cancels across the grid, but \(\cos(8x_j)=1\) at every sample. Thus \(T_8=4.2\pi\), an excess of \(0.2\pi\). With nine equally spaced points neither harmonic aliases into zero frequency, so the integral becomes exact.

Second application

Select a point count from measured spectral decay

For a different periodic signal assume the coefficient envelope is verified with \(C=1\), \(q=1/2\). To keep absolute integral error below \(0.010\), require \(4\pi/(2^N-1)<0.010\). Ten points give a bound \(0.0123\), while eleven give \(0.00614\). The latter is the smallest count certified by this envelope; the actual signal may converge faster through cancellations.

Start from this chapter. Integrate each linear interpolant and add the cell contributions. Compare the resulting trapezoidal bias for a quadratic temperature law and for a linear law on identical nodes.

OneSimulation laboratory · Measure what interpolation does to a heat integral

Measure an integral with finite samples

The energy delivered by a time-varying power input is the area under its history. If readings are available only at a fixed interval, integration must proceed from those samples or from additional physical assumptions. On one panel \([a,a+h]\), the midpoint rule uses \(hf(a+h/2)\), while integration of the chord between the endpoints gives \(h[f(a)+f(a+h)]/2\). Both are exact for affine \(f\). They may nonetheless give opposite errors on the same curved signal, which makes their comparison informative.

Expand about the midpoint \(m\), using \(s=x-m\). Odd powers of \(s\) integrate to zero on \([-h/2,h/2]\). The leading correction to \(hf(m)\) is \(f''(m)h^3/24\). Averaging the endpoint expansions instead gives \(hf(m)+f''(m)h^3/8+O(h^5)\). Subtracting the integral, the leading trapezoidal overestimate is \(+f''(m)h^3/12\), while the midpoint estimate has leading error \(-f''(m)h^3/24\). This expansion requires a smooth integrand. At a known abrupt switch in power, align a panel edge with the switch before applying it.

Increase the order by cancelling a term

Cover a fixed interval with equal panels. There are \(O(1/h)\) local errors of order \(h^3\), giving global second-order accuracy for both rules. Now combine two parts midpoint and one part trapezoid. Their leading second-derivative terms cancel. On a panel of width \(H\), the result is \(H[f(a)+4f(a+H/2)+f(a+H)]/6\), Simpson’s rule. It integrates all cubics exactly: the quadratic error cancels and odd terms about the midpoint cancel by symmetry. For sufficiently smooth data the panel error is \(O(H^5)\) and the composite error is \(O(H^4)\).

Simpson’s formula needs midpoint readings. With an even number of equal subintervals, join consecutive pairs and obtain the familiar alternating weights \(1,4,2,4,\dots,4,1\). Applying those integer weights to unequally spaced observations loses the derivation. In that setting use correctly derived nonuniform weights or the trapezoidal rule panel by panel.

Check the signs with a quartic pulse

Let \(P(t)=t^4\) W with \(t\) measured numerically in seconds over \([0,1]\). The exact energy is \(1/5\) J. With one whole panel, the midpoint estimate is \(1/16=0.0625\) J and the trapezoidal estimate is 0.5 J. Convexity explains why the true integral falls between them. Simpson gives \((0+4/16+1)/6=5/24=0.2083333\) J. Its positive error of \(1/120\) J is much smaller, though the quartic term prevents exactness.

Now split into two Simpson panels. The fourth derivative is constant, so halving panel width reduces the global error by exactly 16. The new estimate is \(0.2+(1/120)/16=0.20052083\) J. In a real measurement, noise can dominate such a small discretisation improvement. A common positive bias of 0.01 W integrates to an extra 0.01 J regardless of panel count. Distinguish uncertainty on the measurements from the discretisation of a known smooth curve. The rod experiment provides a direct check of the trapezoidal error sign and its reduction on mesh refinement. Measure the area under a sampled profile.

Build a Romberg table from nested panels

On a smooth interval the composite trapezoid often admits the even-power expansion \(T(h)=I+c_2h^2+c_4h^4+\cdots\). Combining two grid estimates can remove the first term. Romberg integration repeats that cancellation on successive columns. Set \(R_{j,0}=T(h_0/2^j)\). If column \(m-1\) has leading error proportional to \(h^{2m}\), halving \(h\) divides that term by \(4^m\). Solving the two leading error equations for the common limit gives

\[R_{j,m}=R_{j,m-1}+\frac{R_{j,m-1}-R_{j-1,m-1}}{4^m-1},\qquad j\ge m.\]

The second extrapolation therefore uses 15 in its denominator, where the first extrapolation used three. Existing readings can be reused: halving the step retains the old trapezoid with half its old weight and adds the new midpoint samples with the fine spacing. The \(j\)th row thus needs only the newly introduced points. Each extra cancellation requires enough smoothness for another term in the expansion. A jump, an endpoint singularity or noisy samples may destroy the expected pattern.

To integrate \(1/(1+x)\) on \([0,1]\), the trapezoids with one, two and four panels give \(3/4,\ 17/24,\ 1171/1680\). The two first extrapolations are \(R_{1,1}=25/36\approx0.6944444\) and \(R_{2,1}=1747/2520\approx0.6932540\). Using the second denominator yields \(R_{2,2}=4367/6300\approx0.6931746\). The exact answer is \(\log2\approx0.6931472\), so the error falls from about 0.003877 in the four-panel trapezoid to 0.0000274 after the second cancellation. Altogether only five distinct function locations were used. Compare columns of the same order before interpreting changes along the diagonal as a convergence measurement.

Practise on sheet 2 · Does three-level extrapolation integrate every smooth input exactly?. Further reading · University of California, Berkeley · Math 128A Numerical Analysis, lecture notes · Chapter 4, Numerical Differentiation and Integration, §4.5 Romberg Integration.

Handle a jump with one-sided values

Consider a heater that supplies 4 W until \(t=0.3\) s and 1 W from then to one second. The energy is \(4(0.3)+1(0.7)=1.9\) J. A two-panel trapezoidal rule reading at 0, 0.5 and 1 s sees the values 4, 1 and 1. It predicts \(0.5(4+1)/2+0.5(1+1)/2=1.75\) J. The piecewise linear representation invents a sloping transition lasting half a second.

If the switch time is known, split there and use the left and right limiting powers separately. Each constant segment then integrates exactly, including the correct jump without treating an isolated endpoint as carrying energy. If the switch time itself has uncertainty 0.01 s, the resulting energy uncertainty is 0.03 J because the power level changes by 3 W. Refining a numerical integration grid cannot resolve that timing uncertainty by itself.

Turn known endpoint slopes into a quadrature correction

For a uniform composite trapezoid rule on \([a,b]\), examine a single panel by expanding the integrand symmetrically around its midpoint \(m\). The panel trapezoid value is \(h f(m)+h^3 f''(m)/8+O(h^5)\); integrating that same expansion gives \(h f(m)+h^3 f''(m)/24+O(h^5)\). The excess of the panel trapezoid is therefore \(h^3 f''(m)/12+O(h^5)\). Summing over the panels brings in the midpoint approximation to the integral of \(f''\). Its leading continuous value is \(f'(b)-f'(a)\), which telescopes across the entire interval.

Under a bounded fourth derivative this gives \(T_h-I=h^2[f'(b)-f'(a)]/12+O(h^4)\). Subtracting that boundary term gives a fourth-order corrected rule with exactly the same function samples, provided the endpoint slopes are known accurately. This argument also explains why the uncorrected trapezoid can be unusually accurate for a smooth periodic integrand: matching endpoint slopes sets the leading correction to zero without a special cancellation at each interior point. Matching values alone does not suffice if the periodic extension has a slope jump.

Account for both integration error and slope error

Integrate \(f(x)=x^4\) from zero to one with two equal panels. The three values are \(0,1/16,1\), so \(T_{1/2}=(1/2)(1/16+1/2)=9/32=0.28125\). The true integral is 0.2 and the endpoint derivative difference is four. Subtracting \((1/2)^2(4)/12=1/12\) gives \(19/96\approx0.1979167\). The corrected error is \(-1/480\). Its sign is now opposite to the original error; removing a leading term need not provide an upper bound for the remaining integral.

With four panels the trapezoid value becomes \(113/512\approx0.2207031\). Subtracting \(1/48\) gives about 0.1998698 and error \(-1/7680\). The error magnitude falls by sixteen, exactly the fourth-order factor in this polynomial example. The fourth derivative is constant, so the next terms that could disturb this ratio vanish. For a generic smooth function one would expect only an asymptotic approach toward sixteen.

Now suppose a noisy slope measurement makes the right endpoint derivative too large by 0.12 and the left one is exact. On the four-panel grid the corrected integral is displaced by an additional \(-h^2(0.12)/12=-0.000625\), larger than the discretisation error that remained with exact slopes. On a refinement series with that same systematic slope error, the ultimate error becomes second order once more. The theoretical gain therefore depends on the source and accuracy of the endpoint derivatives. Comparing the two error terms before commissioning derivative measurements gives a concrete sampling decision.

A separate possibility is to estimate the endpoint derivatives from nearby samples. To preserve a fourth-order global correction, the error in their difference should be of order \(h^2\) or smaller. A basic one-sided first difference normally has only first-order accuracy and can introduce an order-three term once multiplied by \(h^2\). A suitable higher-order one-sided approximation may repair that deterministic issue, but measurement noise is amplified when nearby noisy readings are subtracted and divided by their short spacing.

Study the full derivation

Integrate the oscillations analytically and sample the envelope · Function approximation and numerical integration

A sensor model asks for many Fourier integrals of a slowly changing envelope. Direct trapezoids can return a full-scale signal where the true integral nearly cancels. Derive a Filon rule from exact oscillatory moments and find where it needs a different local model.

Problem sheet 2 · Smooth pieces and integrated loads

Build a constrained trajectory, infer a spline curvature, and check quadrature by energy conservation over a measured interval.

Connect two resting positions

A carriage moves from 0 to 4 m in two seconds, starting and finishing at rest. Find the unique cubic trajectory satisfying these four endpoint data. Determine midpoint speed and the two endpoint accelerations. Can this cubic join a stationary segment with continuous acceleration?

Worked solution

With \(s=t/2\) the trajectory is \(4(3s^2-2s^3)\). Differentiating with respect to time gives \(v=12s(1-s)\) m/s, so \(v(1\ {\rm s})=3\) m/s. Acceleration is \(6(1-2s)\) m/s², giving \(+6\) and \(-6\) at the endpoints.

The velocity can join rest continuously. The stationary segment has zero acceleration, so this particular cubic cannot also give a continuous acceleration there. Enforcing both endpoint accelerations adds two constraints, motivating a higher-degree segment.

Identify what a natural endpoint buys

At \(x=0,1,2\) a rod has displacements \(0,2,0\). Construct the first segment of the natural cubic spline and find its slope at 0 and its value at 0.5. Compare with the quadratic through all three data.

Worked solution

The natural curvatures are \(M_0=M_2=0\). The interior equation gives \(4M_1=6(0-4+0)=-24\), so \(M_1=-6\). Integrate \(S''=-6x\) and impose the first two values to get \(S=3x-x^3\). The initial slope is 3 and midpoint value 1.375.

The global quadratic is \(4x-2x^2\), with initial slope 4 and midpoint value 1.5. Both match every measured value. The difference is detectable in unmeasured derivatives and in the interior prediction because the quadratic has nonzero end curvature.

Recover a missing quadratic load

On a unit-width panel a power signal is quadratic. Its endpoint values are 2 W and 6 W; its measured energy over one second is 3 W·s. Infer its midpoint value, compare the midpoint and trapezoidal estimates, and determine the sign of curvature.

Worked solution

Simpson integrates the quadratic exactly, so \(3=(2+4P_m+6)/6\), giving \(P_m=2.5\) W. The midpoint estimate is 2.5 J and the trapezoidal estimate is 4 J. Their weighted combination \((2\cdot2.5+4)/3=3\) J reproduces the measured energy.

The signal midpoint lies below the endpoint chord value 4 W, so the curvature is positive. These conclusions use the stipulated quadratic law; the same three observations cannot uniquely determine an arbitrary power history.

Does three-level extrapolation integrate every smooth input exactly?

For \(f(x)=x^6\) on \([0,1]\), the trapezoids with one, two and four panels are \(1/2,\ 33/128,\ 1421/8192\). Compute the two first-column corrections and the final diagonal value. Compare it with the exact integral and identify the first uncancelled error power.

Worked solution

Removing the quadratic terms gives \(R_{1,1}=(4(33/128)-1/2)/3=17/96\) and \(R_{2,1}=(4(1421/8192)-33/128)/3=893/6144\). Removing the quartic term gives \((16(893/6144)-17/96)/15=55/384\).

The exact value is \(1/7\), so the excess is \(1/2688\). Both cancellations were exact algebraically, but the trapezoid expansion for this polynomial also contains an \(h^6\) term. Three grids remove two independent error coefficients. Exact integration of every smooth function cannot follow from that finite information.

Revisit chapter 4 · Build a Romberg table from nested panels.

Recover an integral using two biased readings

The four- and eight-interval integrals of the quadratic profile are 306.875 and 306.71875 K·m. Assume their leading error is \(Ch^2\). Eliminate \(C\) to estimate the integral and predict the sixteen-interval output. Explain why this elimination is exact for this model on uniform grids but needs a refinement check for arbitrary data.

Work through the solution

Write \(I_h=I+Ch^2\) and \(I_{h/2}=I+Ch^2/4\). Subtract the first relation from four times the second to get \(I=(4I_{h/2}-I_h)/3=306.66667\) K·m. The eight-interval bias is 0.0520833 K·m. Dividing by four gives a sixteen-interval prediction of 306.67969 K·m. Constant curvature makes the trapezoidal error exactly proportional to \(h^2\) for this quadratic. Curvature variation, lack of smoothness or measurement noise can spoil the assumed ratio.

Use the OneSimulation laboratory to check your answer

Chapter 5 · Gaussian quadrature and exactness

Choose quadrature nodes from moment conditions, prove the polynomial exactness of Gauss rules and map a reference rule to a finite element.

From first principles

Choose quadrature nodes by matching moments

A quadrature formula can be designed by asking it to reproduce integrals of successive monomials. On \([-1,1]\), take two nodes at \(\pm a\) with equal weight \(w\), exploiting reflection symmetry of the interval and uniform measure. This model integrates every odd polynomial exactly because its two sampled values cancel. Exactness for the constant and quadratic terms requires

\[2w=\int_{-1}^{1}1\,dx=2,\]
\[2wa^2=\int_{-1}^{1}x^2\,dx=\frac23 .\]

The equations give \(w=1\) and \(a=1/\sqrt3\). By linearity the resulting two-point Gauss rule is exact for every cubic polynomial,

\[\int_{-1}^{1}p(x)\,dx=p(-1/\sqrt3)+p(1/\sqrt3),\]
\[\deg p\le3 .\]

Its nodes lie inside the interval, making it useful when endpoints are expensive or shared information is unavailable. Higher-degree accuracy for an arbitrary function depends on the size of the unresolved part of its shape. Mapping another interval affinely requires scaling both the nodes and the weights by the appropriate coordinate transformation.

Derivation and applications

Derive a two-point rule when one endpoint must be sampled

Suppose a measurement at one endpoint is already required for another purpose. How much polynomial exactness remains if the second sample can be placed freely? On \([0,1]\), fix the first node at zero and place the second at \(a\). Denote their weights \(w_0,w_1\). Exact integration of the first three monomials requires

\[w_0+w_1=1,\]
\[w_1a=\tfrac12,\]
\[w_1a^2=\tfrac13 .\]

Dividing the quadratic condition by the linear one determines \(a=2/3\); back-substitution fixes both weights. This is the two-node left Gauss–Radau rule. Its first missed monomial can be computed directly,

\[Q(f)=\tfrac14 f(0)+\tfrac34 f(2/3),\]
\[\int_0^1x^3\,dx-Q(x^3)=\tfrac14-\tfrac29=\tfrac1{36}.\]

To see what determines the sign of the error for a more general function, interpolate with a quadratic that matches \(f\) at zero and matches both \(f\) and its derivative at the interior node. The quadrature sees the same values as that quadratic, which it integrates exactly. For a function with continuous third derivative the interpolation remainder has a factor \(x(x-2/3)^2\), nonnegative over the interval. Integrating and applying the weighted mean value theorem gives

\[I-Q=\frac{f'''(\xi)}{6}\int_0^1x(x-2/3)^2dx=\frac{f'''(\xi)}{216},\]
\[0<\xi<1 .\]

A convexity assumption alone does not determine the sign here, since the error depends on the third derivative. Scaling the panel length by \(h\) multiplies the third-derivative error coefficient by \(h^4\).

Worked example

Integrate a profile on a physical interval

On \(0\le t\le3\) let a flow rate be \(q(t)=2+t^2\) in consistent units. The Radau nodes are \(0\) and \(2\), and the integral is \(3[(1/4)2+(3/4)6]=15\). Direct integration gives \(6+9=15\). The freely chosen interior station resolves quadratic curvature using only one new measurement.

Second application

Bound the error for a rising exponential

On \([0,1]\) the rule gives \(Q(e^x)=0.25+0.75e^{2/3}=1.71080\), compared with \(e-1=1.71828\). Its error \(0.00748\) is positive. Since \(1\le f'''\le e\), the derived bound brackets the error between \(1/216=0.00463\) and \(e/216=0.0126\), consistent with the direct result.

Three nodes sampling a Gaussian ensemble

An ensemble has normalised density \(p(x)=e^{-x^2}/\sqrt{\pi}\) over the real line. An instrument records the mean of \(\cos(\omega x)\). Its effective response changes from a slowly varying function to a rapidly oscillating one as \(\omega\) increases. Build a quadrature rule that samples the ensemble only three times. The aim is to learn what exactness for polynomials implies for an observable with a variable frequency.

Exploit symmetry and put nodes at \(-c,0,c\), with equal outer weights \(w\) and central weight \(1-2w\). Odd moments vanish automatically. Integration by parts using \(p'(x)=-2xp(x)\) gives ensemble second and fourth moments \(1/2\) and \(3/4\). Matching them imposes \(2wc^2=1/2\) and \(2wc^4=3/4\). Divide the equations to find \(c^2=3/2\). Substitution gives \(w=1/6\), hence central weight \(2/3\). This proves exactness through degree five without needing a table of Gauss–Hermite nodes.

For the instrument response the three readings give \(Q(\omega)=2/3+\cos(c\omega)/3\). For comparison derive the exact transform \(I(\omega)=\int p(x)\cos(\omega x)\,dx\). Differentiating under the integral and once integrating by parts using the derivative of the Gaussian gives \(I'=-\omega I/2\), with \(I(0)=1\). Hence \(I=\exp(-\omega^2/4)\). This independent answer tests the numerical method in the transition from well-resolved response to nearly complete cancellation.

Expand the cosine in even powers near zero. Its constant, quadratic and quartic terms are integrated exactly. The first possible error has order \(\omega^6\); the sixth ensemble moment is \(15/8\), while the rule produces \(9/8\). The difference begins as \(Q-I=\omega^6/960+O(\omega^8)\). This is a local frequency statement. At high frequency the exact response tends to zero, but the discrete sum repeatedly returns to one. Increasing the node count extends the useful frequency range, as the notebook's independent 64-node comparison illustrates.

A sensor that records only these three phase samples cannot distinguish a large carrier with phases differing by integer multiples of a full cycle from zero carrier at the sampling nodes. This is a property of the information supplied to the quadrature, not a failure of the root-finding procedure that produced the nodes. A useful extra observation would be at a coordinate whose phase differs for the two suspected frequencies; merely repeating a measurement at the central node cannot resolve them.

The Gaussian weight in this example has variance one half. If a laboratory reports a Gaussian standard deviation \(\sigma\) in physical units, first set the physical coordinate to \(\sqrt{2}\sigma x\). The carrier in the dimensionless quadrature then becomes \(\sqrt{2}\sigma\omega_{\rm physical}\). Scaling the nodes without scaling the frequency in the reference calculation would compare two different instruments.

Find a nonzero frequency at which all three individual measurements return exactly one and calculate the true ensemble mean there.

Work through the solution

Choose \(\omega=2\pi/c\). Then \(\omega c=2\pi\), so both outer cosines and the central cosine equal one. The true mean is \(\exp[-\pi^2/c^2]=\exp(-2\pi^2/3)\simeq0.00139\). The error here is large despite exactness for five polynomial degrees; the integrand's scale has changed relative to the chosen sampling nodes.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Spend samples where they are most useful

When the integrand is a callable function, quadrature nodes need not coincide with pre-existing sensors. A rule with \(n\) distinct nodes and \(n\) weights has \(2n\) scalar parameters. Can these integrate every polynomial through degree \(2n-1\)? Merely counting parameters does not prove existence. The Gauss construction supplies nodes for a positive weight function on an interval and establishes the exactness by orthogonality.

Start on \([-1,1]\) with a symmetric two-node rule of the form \(w[f(-c)+f(c)]\). Testing the constant function gives \(2w=2\), hence \(w=1\). Symmetry automatically integrates the odd monomials \(x,x^3\). Testing \(x^2\) gives \(2c^2=2/3\), or \(c=1/\sqrt3\). These four independent moment conditions establish exactness on every cubic by linearity. Testing \(x^4\) produces \(2/9\), whereas its integral is \(2/5\). The error appears at degree four exactly as the parameter count suggested.

Prove the general degree claim

Let \(P_n\) be the degree-\(n\) orthogonal polynomial for the prescribed positive integration weight \(w(x)\). Use its \(n\) distinct interior zeros as nodes. Define the weights by integrating the Lagrange basis on these nodes, so every polynomial of degree at most \(n-1\) is already integrated exactly. To treat any polynomial \(f\) of degree at most \(2n-1\), divide it by \(P_n\), giving \(f=P_n q+r\) with \(\deg q,\deg r\leq n-1\).

The integral of \(P_nq\) vanishes by orthogonality, and every quadrature evaluation of that product vanishes because the nodes are zeros of \(P_n\). Both the integral and the rule therefore reduce to the same value for \(r\). This proves exactness. There is a sharp obstruction to one extra degree: \(P_n^2\) is nonnegative, positive except at finitely many points, and all its sampled values vanish. Its positive integral cannot be reproduced by this rule. Positivity of the individual Gauss weights follows by applying exactness to the square of each Lagrange basis polynomial.

Transfer the rule into a physical element

An element \([a,b]\) is obtained by the affine change \(x=m+\ell \xi\), with midpoint \(m=(a+b)/2\), half-length \(\ell=(b-a)/2\), and \(dx=\ell\,d\xi\). Both the node locations and their weights change. For \([2,4]\), the two physical nodes are \(3\pm1/\sqrt3\), with unit physical weights. The integral of \(x^3\) is \((4^4-2^4)/4=60\). Summing the nodal cubes gives \(2\cdot3^3+6\cdot3(1/3)=60\). A missing Jacobian would escape detection on this particular element where \(\ell=1\); test a second element of a different length.

In a linear finite element with constant density, each consistent mass entry integrates a product of two linear basis functions, hence a quadratic. Two Gauss points integrate it exactly. On a unit element the true mass matrix has diagonal \(1/3\) and off-diagonal \(1/6\). Using just the midpoint makes all four entries \(1/4\). The alternating nodal vector \((1,-1)^T\) then has zero discrete mass although its interpolated field has a positive squared integral. This loss of a mode is a practical consequence of insufficient quadrature. With curved mappings or varying coefficients, determine the degree and smoothness of the entire transformed integrand before reusing a quadrature order.

Distinguish a load integral from a stiffness integral

On the same unit element, a constant axial stiffness integrand made from two derivatives of linear shape functions is itself constant. A single midpoint therefore reproduces the stiffness exactly, despite failing for the consistent mass. If \(EA(x)=1+x^2\), the same midpoint now approximates its integral by 1.25, whereas the exact result is \(4/3\). The difference is \(1/12\) in the chosen units. A rule sufficient for one element contribution may thus be insufficient for another in the same analysis.

To select a quadrature order systematically, multiply out the shape functions or their derivatives, coefficient field and mapping Jacobian in the reference coordinate. For discontinuous coefficients, a single smooth polynomial degree may not exist on the entire element. Splitting at the interface, or integrating separate material subcells, then addresses the modelling geometry more directly than increasing the polynomial exactness of one unbroken-panel rule.

Retain both endpoints and derive the best three-point rule

Some instruments already provide readings at the ends of every panel, or adjacent elements need to communicate values at a shared boundary. If endpoints must be quadrature nodes, the unrestricted Gaussian degree \(2n-1\) is generally unavailable. To see the obstruction on \([-1,1]\) with \(n\) nodes including both ends, form the polynomial \((1-x^2)\prod_{\rm interior}(x-x_j)^2\). It vanishes at every evaluation point and is nonnegative throughout the interval. Its true integral is positive but every quadrature rule using just these values returns zero. Its degree is \(2n-2\), so exactness can extend at most through \(2n-3\).

For three symmetric nodes the interior point is zero. Symmetry forces equal endpoint weights \(a\), with central weight \(b\). Reproducing the constants and the square gives \(2a+b=2\) and \(2a=2/3\), so \(a=1/3,\ b=4/3\). Odd powers integrate to zero by symmetry, hence the rule is exact through degree three, attaining its constrained upper bound. Under affine mapping this is the three-node Gauss–Lobatto rule, also known as the single-panel Simpson rule. Its weights and node placement now follow directly from a measurement constraint and a moment calculation.

Contrast endpoint inclusion with free node placement

Consider the local mass entry on one dimensionless interval \([-1,1]\) for a quadratic middle-node shape function \(\phi(x)=1-x^2\) and uniform density. Its exact consistent mass contribution is \(\int\phi^2dx=\int(1-2x^2+x^4)dx=2-4/3+2/5=16/15\). The endpoint-constrained three-node rule sees values \(0,1,0\), giving \(4/3\), an overestimate of \(4/15\), or 25 percent. The discrepancy arises solely from the quartic term. For a quadratic field, reproducing the field at all interpolation nodes does not imply correct integration of its square.

A three-point Gauss rule has free nodes at zero and \(\pm\sqrt{3/5}\), with weights \(8/9,5/9,5/9\). The outer samples have \(\phi^2=(1-3/5)^2=4/25\). Their total contribution is \(2(5/9)(4/25)=8/45\); adding \(8/9\) recovers \(16/15\). In a nodal element the endpoint-constrained rule makes off-diagonal mass entries vanish when basis functions are evaluated only at their own interpolation nodes. This diagonal mass approximation facilitates explicit updates even while changing the exact form.

Selecting either rule is therefore a physical and numerical modelling choice. A modal frequency depends on the ratio of stiffness energy to mass energy, so changing one mass entry may shift computed frequencies even if a static patch test passes. If the density varies across the element, include its degree or smoothness in the integrand before claiming exactness from polynomial degree counting. A discontinuity inside a panel defeats this counting argument until the integration interval is split.

As a check on assembly, sum all rows of the mass matrix. The shape functions form a partition of unity, so the total mass of a constant field is the integral of the density. The nodal diagonal rule reproduces this total for uniform density despite the incorrect middle diagonal entry just calculated. Testing the mass of a constant alone would therefore fail to expose the approximation affecting internal motion.

Does a high polynomial degree resolve a narrow peak?

Integrate \(1/(x^2+a^2)\) on \([-1,1]\). The exact answer is \(2\arctan(1/a)/a\). Use the ordinary Legendre nodes without centring a separate subinterval on the peak; vary only its half-width \(a\).
