Integrating cosine interpolants on nested grids · Function approximation and numerical integration

Chebyshev points turn polynomial interpolation into a cosine series. Derive the exact moments of each basis polynomial, explain aliasing and find a case where two consecutive quadrature grids confidently agree on the wrong answer.

Chapter 10 · Weighted projections and Legendre polynomials

Derive a best mean-square polynomial and determine how a change of weight changes what that polynomial predicts.

From first principles

Project a signal under a specified measurement weight

Choose a nonnegative weight \(w(x)\) and define the squared error of a model \(p\) by integration of \(w|f-p|^2\) over the interval of interest. Assume the candidate basis functions have finite, positive weighted norms and are mutually orthogonal. To find the best coefficient for a basis function \(\phi_j\), vary that coefficient while holding the others fixed. The derivative of the squared error is

\[\frac{\partial}{\partial a_j}\int w\left(f-\sum_k a_k\phi_k\right)^2 dx=-2\left\langle f-\sum_k a_k\phi_k,\phi_j\right\rangle_w.\]

At the minimum this derivative vanishes. All cross terms involving other basis functions disappear by orthogonality, leaving

\[a_j=\frac{\langle f,\phi_j\rangle_w}{\langle\phi_j,\phi_j\rangle_w}.\]

Consequently each coefficient can be computed independently. For unit weight on \([-1,1]\), the Legendre polynomials are one natural choice. If an experiment samples unevenly or certain regions matter more, the implied weight and hence the best fit can change even though the basis spans the same set of functions.

Derivation and applications

Quantify the return from adding one orthogonal basis function

Given a limited coefficient budget, one may want to know exactly how much an extra term reduces integrated squared error. Consider a real signal \(f\), a positive weight defining an inner product, and an existing least-squares projection \(p\) onto a finite-dimensional space. Its residual \(r=f-p\) is orthogonal to every included basis function. Suppose a proposed function \(\psi\) overlaps the original space. Remove its projection first,

\[v=\psi-P\psi,\]
\[\langle v,p_{\rm old}\rangle=0\quad\text{for every }p_{\rm old}\text{ in the original space}.\]

If \(v=0\), the added function contains no new direction. Otherwise the expanded space splits into the original space and the direction \(v\). The new projection is \(p+av\); expansion and minimization give

\[\|r-av\|^2=\|r\|^2-2a\langle r,v\rangle+a^2\|v\|^2,\]
\[a=\frac{\langle r,v\rangle}{\|v\|^2}.\]

The benefit of adding the term follows immediately,

\[\|r\|^2-\|r-av\|^2=\frac{|\langle r,v\rangle|^2}{\|v\|^2}.\]

This expression is unchanged if the candidate is multiplied by a nonzero constant. It also explains why a large raw correlation with a candidate function need not imply new explanatory power: part of that candidate can be represented already. The chosen weight must be used consistently in all projections. A continuous integral and a discrete sum over experimental samples may define different gains.

Worked example

Calculate the gain from adding curvature

With unit weight on \([-1,1]\), fit \(f=x^2\) first by a constant. The constant fit is \(1/3\). Adding \(\psi=x^2\) introduces \(v=x^2-1/3\), equal to the residual. The integrated reduction is \(\int_{-1}^1(x^2-1/3)^2dx=2/5-2/9=8/45\). The expanded fit reproduces the signal exactly.

Second application

Compare two candidate corrections to a measured constant

Take three equally weighted readings \(f=(2,3,7)\). The constant projection gives 4 and residual \((-2,-1,3)\). Two proposed centered shapes are \(u=(-1,0,1)\) and \(v=(1,-2,1)\). Their gains are \(5^2/2=12.5\) and \(3^2/6=1.5\) respectively. The first leaves squared error \(14-12.5=1.5\). The other centered shape is orthogonal to the first, so adding both eliminates the residual.

Distance depends on the observation

A large error over a small part of an interval may be acceptable when an instrument averages over the interval. With a positive integrable weight \(w\), minimise \(J(p)=\int_a^b w(x)|f(x)-p(x)|^2\,dx\) over polynomials of degree at most \(m\). The first variation in an arbitrary allowed direction \(q\) gives \(\int w(f-p)q=0\). Conversely this orthogonality implies \(J(p+q)=J(p)+\int wq^2\). This Pythagorean identity proves uniqueness and explains why a residual with large local extrema can still be optimal in the chosen metric.

In a monomial basis these conditions couple every coefficient to every other. Choose polynomials \(\phi_j\) orthogonal for the same weight instead. Then \(p=\sum c_j\phi_j\) with \(c_j=\int wf\phi_j/\int w\phi_j^2\). Increasing the degree preserves every coefficient already computed. Using quadrature to estimate these integrals introduces another approximation. A rule that samples too sparsely can make two distinct basis vectors look identical under the discrete inner product.

Construct a basis on a symmetric interval

For unit weight on \([-1,1]\), start with \(1,x,x^2\). Odd integrands integrate to zero, so the first two are orthogonal already. Subtract the projection of \(x^2\) onto the constant, namely \(1/3\), and retain \(x^2-1/3\). Scaling this polynomial to take value one at the right endpoint gives \(P_2=(3x^2-1)/2\), the second Legendre polynomial. Its squared integral is \(2/5\), obtained by integrating its expanded fourth-degree square.

Approximating \(x^2\) by a line retains just \(1/3\). Squaring the residual and integrating gives \(2/5-4/9+2/9=8/45\). The mean-square error is half that number and its square root is about 0.29814. The worst local error occurs at the endpoints and equals \(2/3\). The constant \(1/2\) has smaller uniform error but a larger squared integral of \(7/30\). The objective function explains the difference without appealing to how a fitted curve looks.

Move the weight toward one endpoint

On \([0,1]\) choose density \(w(x)=2x\), normalised to integrate to one. Fitting a constant to \(f(x)=x\) now gives its weighted mean \(2\int_0^1 x^2\,dx=2/3\). The weighted mean-square error is \(2\int x^3\,dx-(2/3)^2=1/18\). Equal weighting would instead place the constant at \(1/2\). Selecting a weight thus amounts to specifying the region over which a loss should be economical. When interpreting coefficients state the interval, basis normalisation and weight together; otherwise two reported coefficients can be different descriptions of the same fitted polynomial.

A discrete inner product can give a different optimum

If only the endpoints of \([-1,1]\) are sampled with equal weight, then \(x^2\) takes value one at every observation and the best discrete constant is one. Adding the midpoint with the same weight changes it to \(2/3\). Simpson weights on those three samples instead give \((1+4\cdot0+1)/6=1/3\), the exact continuous coefficient, because that particular integral is quadratic. A mismatch between a discrete fit and a continuous projection can thus arise entirely from the observation design, even with error-free measurements.

Represent multiplication by the independent variable

Orthogonal polynomials supply a bridge from projection to quadrature. Choose an orthonormal family \(\phi_0,\phi_1,\ldots\) on an interval with positive weight \(w\). Multiplication of \(\phi_j\) by \(x\) raises its degree by one. Its inner product with \(\phi_i\) for \(i<j-1\) vanishes, because \(\langle x\phi_j,\phi_i\rangle=\langle\phi_j,x\phi_i\rangle\) and \(x\phi_i\) has degree below \(j\). Consequently \(x\phi_j\) is a linear combination of just \(\phi_{j-1},\phi_j,\phi_{j+1}\). Choosing positive leading coefficients makes the outer recurrence coefficients positive.

Truncating this multiplication map to the first \(n\) basis functions gives a symmetric tridiagonal matrix. At a root \(\xi\) of \(\phi_n\) the omitted last recurrence term vanishes. The column \((\phi_0(\xi),\ldots,\phi_{n-1}(\xi))^T\) is then an eigenvector of the truncated matrix with eigenvalue \(\xi\). Thus a polynomial root-finding task for Gaussian quadrature nodes becomes a symmetric eigenvalue problem. This correspondence uses the same positive weight throughout. If projection coefficients were assembled with a different effective inner product from the quadrature moments, the nodes would describe a different measure.

Derive two-point Gaussian quadrature from a small matrix

On \([-1,1]\) with unit weight, use \(\phi_0=1/\sqrt2\) and \(\phi_1=\sqrt{3/2}\,x\). Both diagonal entries \(\int x\phi_i^2dx\) vanish by oddness. The off-diagonal entry is \((\sqrt3/2)\int_{-1}^1x^2dx=1/\sqrt3\). The truncated multiplication matrix has zeros on the diagonal and \(1/\sqrt3\) off it, so its eigenvalues are \(\pm1/\sqrt3\). The two normalised eigenvectors have first-coordinate magnitude \(1/\sqrt2\). Symmetry makes the quadrature weights equal and their sum must be the mass of the interval, two; hence the two weights are both one.

A further spectral check reconstructs low moments without an integration table. Write the matrix as \(J\). Since \(J^2=I/3\), the quadrature estimate of the second moment is \(2e_1^TJ^2e_1=2/3\), whereas odd moments through three vanish. The rule therefore integrates every cubic exactly by linearity. At degree four the same formula predicts \(2/9\), while the true fourth moment is \(2/5\). Multiplication by \(x\) repeatedly can produce a component outside the retained basis that returns later to affect the observed moment; truncation eventually loses that information.

For nonsymmetric weights the recurrence diagonal is usually nonzero and the quadrature nodes need no longer occur in opposite pairs. A practical diagnostic remains available: all weights must be positive and their sum must equal the integral of the constant. Passing these checks is necessary but does not certify the node positions. Testing further independently known moments can expose a normalisation or indexing error that the constant check alone would miss.

For instance adding the same spurious offset to both computed quadrature nodes leaves the weights positive and unchanged. The integral of the constant still passes exactly, while the first moment becomes twice that offset instead of zero. On an interval invariant under reflection, testing one odd and one even moment quickly distinguishes centring errors from errors in the spacing or overall weight normalisation.

Problem sheet 5 · Spectral measures of error

Distinguish mean-square projections on continuous and sampled intervals and interpret the residual energy.

Recover the invisible frequency

A recorder samples \(\sin(7x)\) at \(x_j=2\pi j/10\). Identify its low-frequency alias. Give a point where the two functions disagree, and find the mean-square energy of their difference over a period.

Worked solution

Since 7 is congruent to −3 modulo 10, the sampled signal agrees with \(-\sin(3x)\). At \(x=\pi/10\) their difference is \(2\sin(3\pi/10)\approx1.618\).

On the continuous period the two sine modes are orthogonal. Each has mean square \(1/2\), so their difference has mean square 1. A vanishing discrepancy at every sample therefore coexists with an order-one continuous error.

Which modes deserve space?

Let \(f(x)=1+0.6\sin x+0.8\cos(4x)\). Compute its mean square, the root-mean-square error after retaining frequencies at most one, and the best additional single real basis function among all higher-frequency sine and cosine modes.

Worked solution

Pairwise orthogonality gives \(1+0.36/2+0.64/2=1.5\). Omitting the fourth cosine leaves root-mean-square error \(\sqrt{0.32}\approx0.566\).

The only omitted nonzero coefficient multiplies \(\cos(4x)\). Adding this mode removes the entire remaining mean-square error. Choosing an intermediate frequency for its lower index would make no change for this specific signal.

Project an odd function

Find the best linear mean-square approximation to \(x^3\) on \([-1,1]\) with unit weight. Compute its squared integrated error and compare the optimum slope with endpoint interpolation.

Worked solution

Symmetry forces zero constant. Orthogonality to \(x\) requires \(a=\int x^4/\int x^2=(2/5)/(2/3)=3/5\).

Expanding the square gives \(2/7-2(3/5)(2/5)+(9/25)(2/3)=8/175\). Endpoint interpolation gives slope 1. The least-squares line trades endpoint accuracy for lower integrated loss.

Find the zeros in an eight-sample spectrum without eight long sums

A sensor returns \((1,2,0,0,1,2,0,0)\) at eight equally spaced times. Before computing any transforms, predict which four Fourier bins vanish. Use a four-point transform to find the other bins and verify the discrete energy identity for the unnormalised DFT.

Worked solution

Pair sample \(n\) with \(n+4\). The second weight differs by \(e^{-i\pi k}=(-1)^k\), so all odd bins cancel. For even \(k=2j\) the two halves reinforce and give twice the four-point DFT of \((1,2,0,0)\). Its entries are \(3,1-2i,-1,1+2i\). Thus \(X_0=6,\ X_2=2-4i,\ X_4=-2,\ X_6=2+4i\).

The sample energy is \(2(1^2+2^2)=10\). The frequency-domain squared magnitudes sum to \(36+20+4+20=80\). Dividing by eight gives 10. Both the zeros and the energy check depend on the adopted transform convention. Reversing the exponent sign conjugates these coefficients and leaves their squared magnitudes unchanged.

Revisit chapter 9 · Factor the discrete Fourier transform into short transforms.

Chapter 11 · Quadrature near singularities and oscillations

Transform integrable endpoint singularities and use phase information to diagnose a misleading quadrature result.

From first principles

Remove an integrable endpoint singularity by substitution

An integrand can be unbounded and still have finite area. Consider \(f(x)=x^{-\alpha}g(x)\) on \([0,1]\), where \(g\) is continuous and bounded and \(0<\alpha<1\). The power law has a finite integral near zero since

\[\int_0^\varepsilon x^{-\alpha}\,dx=\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\alpha}}{1-\alpha}.\]

Ordinary endpoint rules may still try to sample \(f(0)\), which is undefined. Substitute \(x=t^q\) and include the Jacobian \(dx=q t^{q-1}dt\). The transformed integral becomes

\[\int_0^1 x^{-\alpha}g(x)\,dx=\int_0^1 q\, t^{q(1-\alpha)-1}g(t^q)\,dt .\]

Taking \(q=1/(1-\alpha)\) removes the explicit power. For a square-root singularity the familiar substitution \(x=t^2\) gives a regular factor \(2g(t^2)\). Continuity of the transformed integrand makes endpoint evaluation meaningful, while the convergence order of a high-order rule still depends on how many transformed derivatives are bounded.

Derivation and applications

Subtract a known endpoint contribution before integrating the remainder

A substitution is one way to handle an integrable endpoint singularity. If an expansion of its smooth coefficient is known, a second route is to integrate a few singular terms analytically. Consider \(I=\int_0^1g(x)/\sqrt{x}\,dx\) with \(g\) twice continuously differentiable and \(|g''|\le M\). Taylor’s theorem about \(x=0\) separates the constant and slope from a remainder bounded by \(Mx^2/2\),

\[g(x)=g(0)+xg'(0)+R(x),\]
\[|R(x)|\le Mx^2/2 .\]

The first two contributions have finite known moments. The residual now vanishes at the problematic endpoint and can be sampled continuously there,

\[I=2g(0)+\frac23g'(0)+\int_0^1\frac{R(x)}{\sqrt{x}}dx,\]
\[\left|\frac{R(x)}{\sqrt{x}}\right|\le\tfrac M2 x^{3/2} .\]

If only the short initial interval \([0,h]\) is targeted, the two analytic terms scale as \(\sqrt h\) and \(h^{3/2}\). Bounding the remaining contribution yields a direct local error certificate,

\[\left|\int_0^h\frac{g(x)}{\sqrt x}\,dx-2g(0)\sqrt h-\frac23g'(0)h^{3/2}\right|\le\frac{M}{5}h^{5/2}.\]

The two known coefficients have to use the same units and coordinate origin as \(h\). Numerically it can be advantageous to evaluate the residual through a stable expression or a local series, because subtracting its first two terms from a nearly equal value of \(g(x)\) can lose relative precision.

Worked example

Integrate a polynomial endpoint layer

For \(g(x)=1+3x+2x^2\) and \(h=0.04\), the two retained terms give \(0.4+2(0.008)=0.416\). The remaining integral is \(2(2/5)h^{5/2}=0.000256\), giving total \(0.416256\). The error bound with \(M=4\) also equals \(0.000256\), attained because the curvature has constant sign and magnitude.

Second application

Choose an endpoint panel from a tolerance

A different coefficient has verified \(|g''|\le10\). To limit the omitted endpoint area to \(10^{-5}\), require \(2h^{5/2}\le10^{-5}\), or \(h\le(5\times10^{-6})^{2/5}=0.00758\). Choosing \(h=0.005\) gives a bound \(3.54\times10^{-6}\). The remaining regular part of the integral still needs its own quadrature budget.

When derivative bounds fail

The usual trapezoid error bound needs a bounded second derivative. The integral of \(x^{-1/2}\) from zero to one is finite, yet the integrand itself is undefined at the first endpoint. Calling a closed quadrature formula on its original samples is already impossible. Simply omitting that endpoint also omits part of a finite area, and its size scales as the square root of the omitted interval. A substitution can encode the known local behaviour exactly.

Set \(x=t^q\). A factor \(x^\alpha dx\) becomes \(qt^{q(\alpha+1)-1}\,dt\). For \(\alpha>-1\), choosing \(q=1/(\alpha+1)\) removes the leading power. Any additional factor \(g(t^q)\) still determines the transformed smoothness. For \(\alpha=-1/2\), \(q=2\) and \(g(x)=1+x\), the integral becomes \(\int_0^1 2(1+t^2)\,dt=8/3\). Composite Simpson quadrature on just two equal panels is exact on this quadratic. Assign the endpoint of the transformed expression by its limit to avoid evaluating zero times infinity.

A deceptively constant sample

Now consider \(\int_0^1\cos(16\pi x)\,dx\). The exact value is zero, because eight periods fit the interval. At the nine nodes of an eight-panel uniform trapezoid rule each value is one. The computed area is one, a full unit of error for an analytic function. A finer grid of sixteen panels samples alternating signs and returns zero. Repeating the first calculation on four panels also returns one. Agreement between the four- and eight-panel estimates therefore gives a false impression of convergence.

The aliasing follows from summing a geometric progression of sampled complex exponentials. When the phase step is an integer multiple of \(2\pi\), neighbouring samples coincide. Changing the offset slightly can expose the problem even before changing the number of nodes. A bound involving derivatives recognises the difficulty because the second derivative grows quadratically with the phase frequency.

Use cancellation as part of the method

When the oscillatory factor is known analytically, integrate it rather than forcing the grid to resolve every period. For a differentiable amplitude \(g\), integration by parts gives

\[\int_a^b g(x)\cos(\omega x)\,dx=\left[\frac{g(x)\sin(\omega x)}{\omega}\right]_a^b-\frac{1}{\omega}\int_a^b g'(x)\sin(\omega x)\,dx.\]

This identity shows how smooth amplitudes can produce small integrals even when pointwise values are large. For \(g=x\) and \(\omega=\pi\) on \([0,1]\), the boundary term vanishes. The remaining sine integrates to \(2/\pi\), giving \(-2/\pi^2\). A quadrature scheme that approximates the slowly varying amplitude by lines and integrates each line against the phase reproduces this result exactly for this example. If the phase itself is uncertain, that modelling error must be assessed separately from quadrature error.

Test integrability before applying the change

For \(f(x)=1/x\) on \((0,1]\) the substitution \(x=t^q\) leaves \(q/t\), still nonintegrable for every positive exponent. A numerical rule excluding zero may nevertheless return an ordinary finite number. The integral from a cutoff \(\delta\) to one is \(-\log\delta\), so reducing the cutoff by a factor of ten adds \(\log10\approx2.3026\) every time. That persistent change signals a divergent problem definition. It calls for a physically justified finite cutoff or a different mathematical quantity before refining a quadrature rule.

Integrate the singular weight exactly on each panel

Sometimes a substitution changes an integrable singularity into a regular function but also complicates the geometry or an expensive supplied amplitude. An alternative is product integration. Suppose \(I=\int_0^1 x^{-1/2}g(x)\,dx\) where only the smooth amplitude \(g\) is sampled. Replace \(g\) by its piecewise linear interpolant while keeping the weight exactly inside each local integral. The contribution of an endpoint sample equals the integral of the corresponding linear basis function against the singular weight. None of these weights requires evaluating \(x^{-1/2}\) at zero.

On a single panel covering the unit interval the basis functions are \(1-x\) and \(x\). Their weights are \(2-2/3=4/3\) and \(2/3\), respectively. They are positive and sum to two, the exact integral of the singular factor. The rule reproduces constant and linear amplitudes exactly. If \(|g''|\le M\), the pointwise error satisfies \(|g-I_1g|\le Mx(1-x)/2\). Multiplying by the positive weight and integrating gives absolute error at most \((M/2)(2/3-2/5)=2M/15\). This derivation tracks smoothness of the amplitude that is actually interpolated.

Compare a weighted panel with an ordinary endpoint sample

Let \(g(x)=1+x^2\). The analytically integrated value is \(2+2/5=2.4\). The weighted panel predicts \((4/3)1+(2/3)2=8/3\). The excess is \(4/15\), exactly the bound for \(M=2\), because the interpolating line lies above this convex amplitude throughout the open interval. A direct unweighted trapezoid evaluation would have tried to sample an infinite value at the left endpoint. The finite weights here contain all information about the integrable divergence.

For a more refined calculation divide at \(x=1/4\). On the first panel, rescaling \(x=t/4\) multiplies the singular measure by \(1/2\), so the local weights become \(2/3\) and \(1/3\). On the second panel use the basis \((1-x)/(3/4)\), \((x-1/4)/(3/4)\). The weight of its left endpoint is \((4/3)[2\sqrt x-(2/3)x^{3/2}]_{1/4}^1=5/9\). The remaining weight is \(4/9\), since that panel's total singular measure is one. Combining adjacent contributions gives global weights \(2/3,8/9,4/9\) at the three sampled amplitudes.

The estimate is now \(2/3+(8/9)(17/16)+(4/9)2=2.5\). Its positive error has fallen from \(4/15\) to 0.1. Subdivision concentrated near the singular endpoint is one design choice, while error here also depends on where the smooth amplitude bends. If the amplitude itself has a discontinuity, splitting at that physical interface is necessary before invoking the second-derivative bound on either side.

The positive weights also control bounded amplitude uncertainty. If each of the three amplitude evaluations is uncertain by at most 0.01, the worst possible additional integral error is \(0.01(2/3+8/9+4/9)=0.02\). The bound is achieved by a shared positive offset of 0.01. Unlike the deterministic interpolation discrepancy, this worst-case observation error will remain 0.02 under any further positive-weight refinement that still integrates constant amplitudes exactly. A complaint about slow convergence near the endpoint could therefore reflect either amplitude resolution or the data error floor.

Repeated amplitude measurements can reduce independent sampling variance but cannot remove the effect of a shared calibration offset.

Chapter 12 · Monte Carlo integration and stratification

Estimate an integral from random samples, attach a sampling uncertainty and derive the gain from stratification in a simple case.

From first principles

Derive a Monte Carlo estimate and its sampling error

Draw independent, uniformly distributed points \(X_i\) on \([a,b]\), and suppose \(f(X_i)\) has finite variance \(\sigma_f^2\). The expected value of one function sample is its integral divided by the interval length. This immediately gives an unbiased integral estimator,

\[I=(b-a)\operatorname{E}[f(X)],\]
\[\widehat I_N=\frac{b-a}{N}\sum_{i=1}^N f(X_i).\]

For independent draws, the variance of a sum is the sum of the variances. Dividing by the square of the sample count gives

\[\operatorname{var}(\widehat I_N)=\frac{(b-a)^2\sigma_f^2}{N}.\]

Replacing \(\sigma_f\) by the empirical sample standard deviation estimates the standard error. The \(1/\sqrt N\) decrease concerns repeated sampling; it is not a deterministic bound on one estimate. Quadrupling the number of evaluations halves that standard error. Dependence between samples, an infinite variance, or a biased point generator changes the reasoning and the interpretation of the reported uncertainty.

Derivation and applications

Predict when a reflected random sample reduces integration noise

Uniform draws on \([0,1]\) have the same distribution after reflection about \(1/2\). This permits antithetic sampling, in which each independent draw \(U\) is paired with \(1-U\). For a square-integrable integrand \(f\), define the per-pair estimate \(Z\) as the average of its two evaluations. Both terms have the desired integral as their expectation, so

\[Z=\tfrac12[f(U)+f(1-U)],\]
\[\operatorname{E}Z=\int_0^1 f(x)\,dx .\]

Assume \(m\) independently generated pairs and finite single-sample variance \(\sigma^2>0\). The two members within a pair can be correlated. Let \(\rho\) be their correlation. Expanding the variance of their sum gives

\[\operatorname{var}(Z)=\frac{\sigma^2}{2}(1+\rho),\]
\[\operatorname{var}(\overline Z_m)=\frac{\sigma^2}{2m}(1+\rho).\]

For the same \(2m\) evaluations, independent sampling has variance \(\sigma^2/(2m)\); thus negative correlation helps. The source of any reduction can be seen by decomposing \(f\) about the midpoint into symmetric and antisymmetric pieces,

\[f_{\rm even}(x)=\tfrac12[f(x)+f(1-x)],\]
\[f_{\rm odd}(x)=\tfrac12[f(x)-f(1-x)] .\]

Pair averaging keeps the first and cancels the second exactly. The two pieces are orthogonal under uniform integration, so cancellation removes the antisymmetric contribution to variability. It also uses two evaluations for each independent pair. Standard errors should therefore be calculated across the pair averages.

Worked example

Calculate the gain for a quadratic integrand

For \(f(x)=x^2\), \(\sigma^2=1/5-1/9=4/45\). The covariance with its reflected partner is \(\int_0^1x^2(1-x)^2dx-1/9=1/30-1/9=-7/90\), giving \(\rho=-7/8\). At a fixed evaluation budget the variance falls by a factor of eight and the standard error by \(\sqrt8\). Each pair integrates the linear part about the midpoint exactly.

Second application

Recognize when the second evaluation is redundant

For \(f(x)=(x-1/2)^2\), reflection gives the same function value. Here \(\rho=1\) and the paired method has twice the variance of independent sampling at equal cost. With 200 evaluations and single-value variance \(1/180\), the independent standard error is \(1/\sqrt{36000}=0.00527\); using 100 independent pairs gives \(1/\sqrt{18000}=0.00745\).

Turn area into an expectation

For an integrable \(f\) on \([a,b]\), let \(U\) be uniform on the interval. Then \(I=(b-a)\mathbb E[f(U)]\). Independent draws give an unbiased estimator by averaging their function values and multiplying by the length. With finite variance \(\sigma^2=\operatorname{Var}(f(U))\), independence eliminates cross-covariances and gives \(\operatorname{Var}(\hat I)=(b-a)^2\sigma^2/n\). The typical size of the sampling error decreases with the square root of the sample count. Multiplying the budget by four halves the standard error.

In a numerical run replace the unknown variance by the sample variance with denominator \(n-1\). For a sufficiently large sample with a suitable distribution, a normal approximation provides an approximate confidence interval. A particular interval need not contain the exact answer. Heavy tails, correlated draws or a nearly unsampled small peak can make the estimated uncertainty unreliable. Recording the random seed reproduces the same sequence of sample values; independent seeds are needed for independent replications.

Four draws and the uncertainty they reveal

For \(f(x)=x\) on \([0,1]\), four illustrative draws at 0.1, 0.2, 0.7 and 0.8 have mean 0.45. Their squared deviations sum to 0.37, yielding sample variance \(0.37/3\) and estimated standard error \(\sqrt{0.37/12}\approx0.176\). The exact integral is 0.5, so the observed error is 0.05. Because the uniform variance is exactly \(1/12\), the true standard deviation for repeated sets of four draws is \(\sqrt{1/48}\approx0.144\). These three numbers answer different questions about this one run and its possible replications.

Guarantee some coverage before sampling

Divide the interval into \(n\) equal strata and draw one independent point uniformly inside each. Sum each sample value times its stratum width. The estimator remains unbiased since each local expectation equals the mean over its stratum. For \(f(x)=x\) every local variance is \(1/(12n^2)\). Multiplying by the squared width and summing \(n\) independent contributions gives a total variance of \(1/(12n^3)\). For four samples the standard deviation drops to about 0.0361. The gain comes from fixing the amount of sampling effort in each region.

A single sample per stratum cannot estimate its own local variance. Independent repetitions of the complete stratified procedure or more samples per stratum permit empirical uncertainty estimates. In high dimension a full Cartesian partition requires rapidly growing numbers of cells. Simple Monte Carlo retains the same square-root rate under finite variance, but the variance constant and the cost of sampling can still depend strongly on dimension. Identify both the estimator and the method used to measure its uncertainty when reporting a numerical integral.

Use a known integral to reduce variability

For \(f(x)=x+x^2\), the integral of the first term is known to be one half. Sampling only \(x^2\) and adding one half leaves the expectation unchanged. The original sample variance per uniform draw is \(1/3+1/2+1/5-(5/6)^2=61/180\). The residual sample variance per draw is \(1/5-(1/3)^2=4/45\), less than one third as large. This control-variate construction uses a known mean and a correlation with the original integrand. An arbitrary replacement by a superficially similar function without restoring its known mean would bias the estimate.

Sample more often where the integrand is large

For a nonuniform positive probability density \(q\) on the integration region, the identity \(\int f(x)\,dx=\mathbb E_q[f(X)/q(X)]\) holds whenever the sampling distribution covers the integrand's support. Independent samples produce an unbiased estimate. Its variance per draw is \(\int f(x)^2/q(x)\,dx-I^2\), provided this integral is finite. A density concentrated near large contributions can reduce this variance even if generating a nonuniform draw costs little more than generating a uniform one. A density that is too small where the integrand is large can have the opposite effect.

Cauchy–Schwarz applied to \(\int |f|=\int (|f|/\sqrt q)\sqrt q\) gives \((\int|f|)^2\le\int f^2/q\). Equality requires \(q\) proportional to \(|f|\). For a nonnegative integrand that ideal density makes the sample weight constant and the variance zero. Constructing its normalising constant would generally require knowing the integral itself. The result is thus a design guide: choose a tractable density approximating the shape of the large contributions, with known normalisation and sufficiently broad coverage.

Restore finite variance for an integrable endpoint peak

Take \(f(x)=x^{-1/2}\) on \((0,1]\). The integral is two. Under uniform sampling each weight is finite almost surely and has that finite mean, but its second moment is \(\int_0^1 x^{-1}dx=\infty\). The familiar standard-error formula based on a finite variance and a square-root sampling rate is therefore unjustified, even if a modest run happens to avoid tiny \(x\) and reports a reassuringly small sample variance.

Choose \(q(x)=(3/4)x^{-1/4}\). Its cumulative distribution is \(x^{3/4}\), so one draws \(X=U^{4/3}\) for a uniform \(U\). The importance weight is \(W=(4/3)X^{-1/4}\). The second moment is \((4/3)\int_0^1 x^{-3/4}dx=16/3\), giving variance \(16/3-4=4/3\). For 300 independent draws the exact standard deviation of the mean is \(\sqrt{(4/3)/300}=1/15\). This result quantifies an uncertainty that uniform sampling could not express through a finite variance.

For the ideal comparison \(q_\ast=x^{-1/2}/2\), the cumulative distribution is \(\sqrt x\); generating \(X=U^2\) gives \(W=2\) on every draw. Change the integrand to \(x^{-1/2}(1+x)\) while retaining this density and the weights become \(2(1+X)\), bounded between two and four. The method still controls the singular behaviour without needing exact knowledge of the new integral. For a sign-changing integrand, even sampling from \(|f|\) retains sign fluctuation and cannot eliminate cancellation between large positive and negative contributions.

To check the improved density numerically, record both the sample mean and the largest weight across independent replications. A computation that clips extremely small sampled values of \(X\) to avoid a large weight changes the target expectation. Under the proposed \(q\), the omitted integrand area from zero to a cutoff \(\delta\) is \(2\sqrt\delta\). Even a cutoff of \(10^{-4}\) removes 0.02 integral units, which is already thirty percent of the quoted standard error for 300 samples. Tail handling is therefore part of defining the estimator.

A stable implementation can generate the weight directly from the sampled uniform variable and check this omitted-area estimate.

Compute the expected benefit of stratifying a smooth integrand

Estimate \(\int_0^1 x\,dx\) by sampling function values. A uniform draw has variance 1/12; a draw restricted to a stratum of width \(1/N\) has variance \(1/(12N^2)\). Combine \(N\) independent equal-weight draws in each design.

Problem sheet 6 · Choosing where to sample

Exploit endpoint structure, test aliasing and design a stratified random estimate with a known variance.

Remove a singular factor

Evaluate \(\int_0^1 x^{-2/3}(1+x)\,dx\) using \(x=t^q\). Select \(q\), derive the transformed integrand, and decide whether one Simpson panel pair gives the exact integral.

Worked solution

To neutralise the power require \(q(1-2/3)-1=0\), giving \(q=3\). Then \(x^{-2/3}dx=t^{-2}3t^2dt=3dt\) and the finite endpoint value is 3.

Integrating \(3(1+t^3)\) gives \(3+3/4=3.75\). Simpson is exact on cubics; its three samples are \(3,3.375,6\), and their weighted sum divided by six is 3.75.

Expose a false agreement

For \(f(x)=\cos(12\pi x)\) on \([0,1]\), find the trapezoid results with 3 and 6 panels. Test 12 panels and explain why the initial equal answers cannot justify a zero error estimate.

Worked solution

With three panels the phase advances \(4\pi\) at each sample; with six it advances \(2\pi\). Both return the constant sample average 1.

With twelve panels samples alternate between 1 and −1. The half-weight endpoints together count as one positive value, so all contributions cancel. The exact integral is zero. Correlated aliasing on the first two grids invalidates a smooth-error extrapolation.

Two strata with different variability

A response has variance 1 on the first half of \([0,1]\) and 4 on the other half. Estimate the integral from twelve independent evaluations. Compare the variances for allocations (6,6) and (4,8). Derive the continuous optimum under the same fixed budget.

Worked solution

Each stratum mean has variance \(\sigma_i^2/n_i\) and weight \(1/2\), so total variance is \((1/n_1+4/n_2)/4\). Equal allocation gives \(5/24\); (4,8) gives \(3/16\), slightly smaller.

Differentiating \(1/n_1+4/(12-n_1)\) gives \(-1/n_1^2+4/(12-n_1)^2\). Its zero requires \(12-n_1=2n_1\). Positive second derivative yields the optimum (4,8), allocating in proportion to each stratum's standard deviation.

Choose a quadrature variable before choosing a rule

Estimate the integral of exp(−x)/√x from 0 to 1. A composite trapezoidal rule in x fails at its first endpoint. Remove the singularity by substitution, compute Simpson estimates using two and four panels in the new variable, and derive a practical error estimate. Explain why fitting a polynomial to the original endpoint would change the question.

Worked solution

Set x = t². The Jacobian 2t cancels 1/√x for t positive, and the integrand extends smoothly to g(0) = 2. The integral is now the integral of 2 exp(−t²) over [0,1]. The endpoint value is a limit implied by the model and the substitution.

With two equal panels Simpson gives [g(0)+4g(0.5)+g(1)]/6 ≈ 1.49436. With four panels the weights are 1,4,2,4,1 times 1/12, giving about 1.49371. If the leading error is proportional to h⁴, the estimated fine-grid error magnitude is |S₄−S₂|/15 ≈ 0.000043.

The exact benchmark √π erf(1) ≈ 1.4936483 shows that the fine-grid estimate is already close; the asymptotic estimate is approximate on these coarse grids. Replacing the infinite original endpoint by a fitted finite number amounts to imposing an arbitrary sampling model. The substitution preserves the exact integral and also explains why a higher-order rule now has the smoothness it needs.

Chapter 13 · Evaluating an interpolant without rebuilding its polynomial

Derive a quotient from the Lagrange basis, evaluate the removable singularity at a data point and separate rounding error from sensitivity to the data.

One product shared by all the basis functions

Let distinct nodes \(x_0,\ldots,x_n\) carry data \(y_j\). The cardinal function \(\ell_j(x)\) is one at node \(j\) and zero at the other nodes. Its product formula is expensive if every evaluation repeats the same differences. Set \(w_j=1/\prod_{k\ne j}(x_j-x_k)\) and \(P(x)=\prod_k(x-x_k)\). Away from all nodes, \(\ell_j(x)=P(x)w_j/(x-x_j)\). The polynomial interpolating constant data equal to one is one everywhere. Dividing the interpolating polynomial by this partition of unity cancels \(P(x)\) to give

\[p(x)=\frac{\sum_j w_jy_j/(x-x_j)}{\sum_j w_j/(x-x_j)}.\]

At a node the written quotient divides infinities. Multiply numerator and denominator by \(x-x_j\) and take the limit to recover \(y_j\) exactly. An implementation should return the stored value when the evaluation coordinate equals a node. Multiplying every weight by the same nonzero factor leaves the quotient invariant, a useful freedom when storing weights.

Worked interpolation and data sensitivity

Take the nodes \(-1,0,1\). Their weights are proportional to \(1,-2,1\). With data \(1,0,1\) at \(x=0.5\), the numerator is \(2/3-2=-4/3\) and the denominator is \(2/3-4-2=-16/3\). Hence \(p(0.5)=1/4\), as the polynomial \(x^2\) requires.

Change the middle datum from 0 to 0.01. Its cardinal function is \(1-x^2\), so the prediction at 0.5 rises by 0.0075. For arbitrary data perturbations bounded by \(\delta\), the triangle inequality gives \(|\Delta p(x)|\le\delta\sum_j|\ell_j(x)|\). Large values of this sum signal a sensitivity of the interpolation problem. Evaluating the same polynomial by a smarter formula cannot remove this source of error.

Put more sensors near the endpoints

Approximate \(f(x)=1/(1+25x^2)\) on \([-1,1]\). Both curves use the same number of exact samples and the same barycentric algorithm. One uses equally spaced nodes and the other uses projections of equally spaced angles on a semicircle. Check the interior before inspecting the ends.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on what the formula guarantees

Why do three weights with alternating signs produce an answer between zero and one in the first calculation? Must it always lie between the smallest and largest data?

Worked solution

The example reconstructs \(x^2\), which stays within that range on this interval. General cardinal functions may be negative. Unlike a convex combination, their weighted sum can overshoot the sampled range.

For the three nodes above, perturb only the datum at \(-1\) by 0.04. What is the change at \(x=0.5\)?

Worked solution

The left cardinal polynomial is \(x(x-1)/2\), equal to \(-1/8\) at \(0.5\). The change is \(-0.005\), with the opposite sign to the perturbation.

Compare the construction of weights with the theorem on the barycentric form. Reuse the same nodes to reproduce the data-perturbation calculation. Driscoll and Braun · Barycentric interpolation

Chapter 14 · When a trapezoidal sum counts the wrong frequency perfectly

Use a finite geometric series to explain why a very accurate periodic quadrature rule can completely miss an oscillation.

Summing one Fourier mode

A smooth closed curve parameterised by angle provides a natural periodic integral. On \(N\) equally spaced points \(\theta_j=2\pi j/N\), write the trapezoidal estimate of a mean as \(T_N=N^{-1}\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}f(\theta_j)\). The endpoint \(2\pi\) equals zero angle and is counted only once. For \(f=e^{ik\theta}\), put \(q=e^{2\pi i k/N}\). If \(q\ne1\) then \((1-q)(1+q+\cdots+q^{N-1})=1-q^N=0\). If \(q=1\) all \(N\) samples equal one.

For a Fourier series whose absolute convergence permits termwise summation, this gives

\[T_N=\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb Z}\widehat f_{\ell N},\]
\[T_N-\widehat f_0=\sum_{\ell\ne0}\widehat f_{\ell N}.\]

The continuous mean sees only the constant mode. The grid silently adds every integer multiple of its sample count to that mode. Fast decay of smooth Fourier coefficients usually makes this error small. A single deliberately aligned harmonic can defeat the rule.

Two rings of sensors

A ring carries the temperature \(20+3\cos(8\theta)\) °C. Eight sensors at zero angular offset all read 23 °C. The true mean is 20 °C. Nine equally spaced sensors give the exact mean for this harmonic. Moving the eight sensors through an offset \(\phi\) changes their mean to \(20+3\cos(8\phi)\) while the ring is unchanged.

A different signal \(2+\cos(3\theta)+0.2\cos(10\theta)\) on five sensors gives 2.2 because the tenth harmonic aliases to zero frequency. Increasing the number to ten gives the same wrong answer. Independent node offsets or a noncommensurate sample count can challenge an apparently converged result.

Move the sampling ring

The curve gives the mean reconstructed from rotated sensors over one revolution. Change the number of sensors and the physical harmonic separately. Predict the counts that will make the measurement depend on the angular offset.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on resolution

A discrete test gives the exact mean of \(\cos(7\theta)\) on eight equally spaced nodes. Can eight samples reconstruct that frequency uniquely?

Worked solution

Its mean vanishes, but samples of \(\cos(7\theta)\) coincide with those of \(\cos(\theta)\) on this grid. Accurate integration of the mean does not imply unique reconstruction.

Average two \(N\)-point rules offset by \(\pi/N\). Which aliases survive?

Worked solution

Together the rings form a \(2N\)-point uniform grid. Terms at odd multiples of \(N\) change sign between grids and cancel. Terms at even multiples survive.

Chapter 15 · Deriving Romberg integration and recognising a broken error model

Build an extrapolation table from nested trapezoidal sums and explain when cancelling a presumed leading error makes a poor estimate look precise.

Cancelling a nuisance coefficient

Suppose a smooth integrand gives \(T(h)=I+ah^2+bh^4+O(h^6)\). Halving the mesh gives \(T(h/2)=I+ah^2/4+bh^4/16+O(h^6)\). Multiply the second formula by four, subtract the first and divide by three. The unknown \(a\) disappears without being estimated explicitly. The new leading error is \(-bh^4/4\).

At level \(j\) a smooth even-power expansion starts with a power \(h^{2j}\) to be removed from the preceding column. The same two-row elimination yields

\[R_{k,0}=T(2^{-k}h_0),\]
\[R_{k,j}=R_{k,j-1}+\frac{R_{k,j-1}-R_{k-1,j-1}}{4^j-1}.\]

Each refinement reuses all previous function values. Add only the midpoints of old panels, because every old endpoint becomes an even-indexed fine node. This is especially attractive when obtaining a sample means running an expensive smooth simulation.

Check the arithmetic twice

For \(\int_0^1 x^4\,dx=1/5\), the first three trapezoidal estimates are 0.5, 0.28125 and 0.220703125. The first extrapolation column contains 0.2083333 and 0.20052083. Combining them with coefficient 15 gives 0.2. This is an exact polynomial check on indexing and column orientation.

For \(\int_0^1\sqrt{x}\,dx=2/3\), the first two trapezoidal values are 0.5 and approximately 0.603553. Their extrapolate is about 0.63807. The derivative is singular at zero. The endpoint panel alone has an error proportional to \(h^{3/2}\), the same scale that dominates the global error. A factor of four cannot cancel it.

Change the regularity at the endpoint

Integrate \(x^p\) on the unit interval. Read absolute error for the raw trapezoidal estimates and their first extrapolation. The ordinate is the base-ten logarithm, with roundoff-level results displayed at the stated floor. Compare a smooth integer exponent and a fractional one.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on interpreting a convergence table

Given three successive estimates with differences 0.008 and 0.002, what observed order do they suggest on uniform bisection?

Worked solution

A factor of four between signed differences is consistent with \(2^p=4\), hence order two. A further refinement tests whether this is an asymptotic regime.

Remove the square-root difficulty by a change of variable. What estimate becomes exact after one extrapolation?

Worked solution

Set \(x=t^2\), \(dx=2t\,dt\). The integral becomes \(\int_0^1 2t^2\,dt\). The raw trapezoidal error for a quadratic has only an \(h^2\) term, so first-column Richardson extrapolation is exact in exact arithmetic.

Chapter 16 · Summing a Chebyshev series by working backwards

Derive a stable evaluation scheme directly from a three-term polynomial identity and diagnose the failure that occurs when a fitted law is used outside its calibrated interval.

From cosines to a polynomial recurrence

Set \(x=\cos\theta\) and \(T_k(x)=\cos(k\theta)\). The angle-addition formula gives \(T_{k+1}=2xT_k-T_{k-1}\), with \(T_0=1,T_1=x\). Consider \(p=\sum_{k=0}^n c_k T_k\) with the constant coefficient included in full. We could generate all the polynomials forward. Clenshaw evaluation instead gathers their weighted coefficients from the high end, without converting to monomials.

Set \(b_{n+1}=b_{n+2}=0\) and \(b_k=c_k+2xb_{k+1}-b_{k+2}\) for \(k=n,\ldots,1\). Substitute \(c_k=b_k-2xb_{k+1}+b_{k+2}\) into the original sum. Every interior \(b_j\) multiplies \(T_j-2xT_{j-1}+T_{j-2}=0\). Only two boundary terms survive:

\[p(x)=c_0+xb_1-b_2.\]

The derivation also identifies a common convention error. If the source defines its zeroth coefficient with a half-weight then it must be halved once before using this formula.

Two useful arithmetic checks

For coefficients \((1,2,3)\) at \(x=0.5\), \(b_2=3\), \(b_1=2+2(0.5)3=5\). The answer is \(1+0.5(5)-3=0.5\). Independently \(T_2(0.5)=-0.5\), giving \(1+1-1.5=0.5\).

On the calibration interval each \(|T_k|\le1\), so an isolated coefficient error of 0.001 changes the value by at most 0.001. At \(x=1.2\), \(T_{10}(1.2)=\cosh(10\operatorname{arcosh}(1.2))\) is around 252. The same tiny coefficient error now changes a prediction by roughly a quarter of a unit. Accurate summation does not make extrapolation reliable.

Follow one uncertain coefficient

A simple calibration \(1+0.4x\) has an uncertain extra coefficient at a selectable order. Find the extreme differences inside the interval, then inspect the growth just beyond the right endpoint. The calculation uses the backward recurrence at every position.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on mapping a laboratory interval

A calibration covers \(10\le T\le50\) °C. Which coordinate should enter the recurrence and what does \(T=54\) correspond to?

Worked solution

Centre at 30 and divide by the half-width 20, so \(x=(T-30)/20\). At 54 °C this gives 1.2, already outside the bounded Chebyshev interval.

Give a computable uniform error bound if three stored coefficients have errors no larger than 0.002 each.

Worked solution

For \(|x|\le1\), the triangle inequality and \(|T_k|\le1\) give 0.006. Exact values may cancel at some positions; this bound does not rely on such cancellation.

Chapter 17 · Where should a random quadrature spend its samples?

Derive the weight that corrects a nonuniform sampling density and see why an unbiased quadrature can nevertheless have infinite variance.

Change the experiment while preserving its mean

For \(I=\int_0^1 f(x)\,dx\), choose a probability density \(p(x)>0\) wherever \(f\ne0\). If \(X\) is drawn from \(p\), then averaging \(W=f(X)/p(X)\) gives \(E[W]=\int (f/p)p=I\). With independent draws and finite second moment, the estimator variance is \([\int f^2/p-I^2]/N\). Merely changing the point distribution without changing the weight would compute a different integral.

For nonnegative \(f\), Cauchy–Schwarz gives \((\int f)^2\le(\int f^2/p)(\int p)\). Equality holds if \(p\) is proportional to \(f\). The ideal distribution would give identical weights, although constructing its exact normaliser frequently requires already knowing the answer. It remains a guide to choosing an approximate density.

A finite expectation with an infinite error bar

For \(f(x)=x^{-1/2}\) the integral is 2. Uniform sampling produces weights \(X^{-1/2}\) whose second moment is \(\int_0^1 x^{-1}\,dx=\infty\). The estimate has the right expected value, but its usual finite-variance standard error has no foundation.

Take \(p_a(x)=(a+1)x^a\) with \(a>-1\). An inverse-CDF draw is \(X=U^{1/(a+1)}\) for a uniform \(U\). For \(f=x^b\), the second moment is \(1/[(a+1)(2b-a+1)]\) when \(2b-a>-1\). At \(b=a=-1/2\), \(X=U^2\) and every weight equals exactly two. At \(b=-1/2,a=-1/4\), the second moment is \(1/(0.75\times0.25)=5.3333\). The variance is 1.3333 and 100 independent draws have standard deviation about 0.1155.

Compare sampling plans on the same draws

Use the same fixed pseudo-random uniform sequence for the two plans so changes reflect the transformation. Sweep the proposal exponent through the finite-variance boundary and inspect the running mean. One finite trajectory can look benign even when the theoretical second moment diverges.

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Tutorial on signs and diagnostics

If \(f\) changes sign, can sampling proportional to \(|f|\) remove every fluctuation?

Worked solution

Then the weight is a constant magnitude multiplied by the sign of \(f\). Its variance vanishes only if that sign is constant almost everywhere. Cancellation between positive and negative contributions can still make relative accuracy poor.

A proposal has zero density on a short interval where \(f\) is nonzero. Does increasing the number of samples repair the estimate?

Worked solution

No sample can reach that interval, so the corresponding integral contribution is absent. This is a bias from missing support; the derivation of \(E[W]=I\) fails there.

Study the full derivation

Spend random samples where they reduce uncertainty · Function approximation and numerical integration

A Monte Carlo estimate can be unbiased and still too noisy to be useful. Derive control variates, allocate a fixed budget across strata, and distinguish variance reduction from an underestimated standard error.

Chapter 18 · Integrating an infinite endpoint with a finite table of samples

A density rises without bound near the edge of a specimen, yet the total inferred amount can be finite. Choose nodes that measure the edge contribution accurately. Derive the tanh–sinh transformation, split two different quadrature errors and detect when refining a grid can no longer answer the physical question.

Determine whether a finite measurement exists

Let \(x\) be the distance from the edge divided by the specimen width. Take the idealised density \(f(x)=x^{-\alpha}\) on the open interval between zero and one. The exponent is a known input in this experiment. Start with \(0<\alpha<1\). If the instrument samples exactly at zero, its readout would be undefined in this model. That does not make the integrated signal infinite. The contribution of the first \(\varepsilon\) of the interval is found by integrating from a positive lower bound and taking that bound to zero.

\[I=\frac{1}{1-\alpha},\]
\[I_{[0,\varepsilon]}=\frac{\varepsilon^{1-\alpha}}{1-\alpha}.\]

This sets the physical scale of the numerical task. With \(\alpha=1/2\), the first one ten-thousandth of the spatial window contributes one per cent of the complete signal. For \(\alpha=0.9\), the same strip contributes almost forty per cent. An instrument with finite edge resolution may require a separate sub-resolution material model. In the numerical exercise the stated power law is exact down to arbitrarily small distance. That assumption permits an independent reference answer and lets us attribute a mismatch to the quadrature rather than to unseen edge physics.

A uniform midpoint rule avoids the forbidden endpoint, using \(h\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}f((j+1/2)h)\) with \(h=1/N\). The exact contribution of the first cell is \(h^{1-\alpha}/(1-\alpha)\). Its midpoint approximation is \(2^\alpha h^{1-\alpha}\). The leading error already visible in this cell decays slowly when the exponent is close to one. Since the density is convex, every midpoint panel underestimates its integral. The errors do not cancel the missing first-cell contribution. The usual smooth-function bound in terms of a uniform second derivative cannot be applied here.

Build a transformation that resolves both ends

Map a real parameter \(t\) onto the physical interval. A convenient first choice is a logistic map, which approaches either endpoint exponentially in the parameter. To concentrate the nodes more aggressively near the endpoints, first replace that parameter by \(\pi\sinh t\). Written using a hyperbolic tangent, the resulting map is the usual tanh–sinh transformation. Its derivative becomes small at precisely those points where the original density becomes large.

\[x(t)=\frac{1}{1+\exp[-\pi\sinh(t)]}=\frac{1+\tanh[(\pi/2)\sinh(t)]}{2},\]
\[x'(t)=\pi\cosh(t)\,x(t)[1-x(t)].\]

Apply the change of variables before choosing any quadrature rule. The new integrand is \(g(t)=\pi\cosh(t)x(t)^{1-\alpha}[1-x(t)]\), integrated along the entire real line. To inspect its left tail take \(t=-u\) with \(u\) large. Then \(\sinh u\) and \(\cosh u\) both grow approximately as \(e^u/2\), while \(x(-u)\) behaves as \(\exp[-(\pi/2)e^u]\). Their product decays like a factor proportional to \(e^u\) multiplied by \(\exp[-(1-\alpha)(\pi/2)e^u]\). The second exponential dominates eventually. As \(\alpha\) approaches one its coefficient shrinks and the relevant left tail extends farther out.

At the right end \(x(t)\) approaches one and the factor \(1-x(t)\) supplies the rapid decay. A symmetric truncation in \(t\) therefore covers two generally unequal tail contributions. On a real density that is singular at both ends, repeat the asymptotic argument separately for each endpoint. For a strong interior peak the present transformation has no reason to concentrate near the peak. Splitting the interval at a known interior singularity would give each transformed subproblem a meaningful endpoint.

Distinguish tail loss from the panel approximation

Choose a finite half-window \(T\), then a step \(h_t\) such that a whole number of panels fits on either side of zero. The notebook applies the composite trapezoid with half weights on both boundary nodes of \([-T,T]\). This calculation leaves out the two infinite tails even if the finite interval is integrated perfectly. There is a second error from replacing the finite integral by sampled trapezoids. At fixed window the panel errors can become tiny while an appreciable area is still missing.

\[I-Q_{T,h_t}=\int_{-\infty}^{-T}g(t)\,dt+\int_T^\infty g(t)\,dt+\left[\int_{-T}^T g(t)\,dt-Q_{T,h_t}\right].\]

In the power-law test the omitted tails can themselves be integrated explicitly. Write \(a=x(-T)\) and \(b=x(T)\). Their sum is \([a^{1-\alpha}+1-b^{1-\alpha}]/(1-\alpha)\). With \(\alpha=0.65,T=3,h_t=0.25\), there are 25 evaluations and the notebook gives about 2.85707045 against the reference 2.85714286. The omitted tail is approximately \(4.70\,10^{-5}\), while the total difference is about \(7.24\,10^{-5}\). The gap between these values belongs to resolution inside the chosen window. Recalculating at smaller \(h_t\) without increasing \(T\) tests only the latter.

Compute the weighted integrand before rounding the endpoints

If \(x\) is stored as exactly one, the expression \(1-x\) loses a small positive number. If \(x\) underflows to zero, multiplying \(f(x)=\infty\) by a derivative rounded to zero causes another failure. Both effects are artefacts of evaluation order. Here the product \(f(x(t))x'(t)\) has a simple algebraic form that is finite everywhere at finite \(t\). Working in logarithms keeps the two endpoint distances separately.

Put \(s=\pi\sinh(t)\). A stable evaluation of \(\log x\) is \(-\log(1+e^{-s})\), and that of \(\log(1-x)\) is \(-\log(1+e^s)\). The notebook calls NumPy's log-add-exp operation on zero and the respective exponent to avoid directly forming a very large exponential. The log of the complete integrand is now the sum of \(\log\pi+\log\cosh t\), \((1-\alpha)\log x\) and \(\log(1-x)\). Exponentiating this final quantity may underflow in sufficiently distant tails. There that represents a negligible weighted contribution, rather than the disappearance of the physical interval itself.

In an integral supplied by a black-box material law the algebraic combination may be less accessible. If the law can accept a distance and the logarithm of a distance, the same strategy works. If measurements stop before either endpoint, no quadrature transformation recovers information about the missing function automatically. A defensible result should record where the model was extrapolated as well as how the integration was performed.

Design two distinct convergence experiments

First vary the number of transformed panels at fixed half-window and plot the absolute error. Use absolute error near a zero integral because a relative percentage becomes ill-conditioned as the answer tends to zero. Next increase \(T\) while keeping the transformed step approximately fixed. More points are then added to the previously discarded tails. If changing the half-window matters and halving the step does not, the initial allocation spent too much effort in the centre. Repeat this reasoning before making claims about asymptotic convergence from only two almost identical values.

For this particular reference family there is an even simpler independent calculation: map \(x=u^{1/(1-\alpha)}\) on the unit interval. The diverging density and the vanishing derivative combine into the constant \(1/(1-\alpha)\). A single properly weighted node can integrate a constant exactly. This yields a stringent check of the implementation while illustrating a general design principle. When the singularity exponent is known and the remaining factor is smooth, a tailored substitution may be the easiest route to the required integral. Tanh–sinh is especially attractive when the two endpoints need protection and their detailed local structure is not fully known.

Compare two uses of a fixed sample budget

Take 33 transformed nodes and put 16 panels on either side of the origin. A half-window of two now gives a step of one eighth. Doubling the half-window with the same number of samples doubles the spacing while extending the tails. There is no general reason this second calculation should be more accurate. It trades centre resolution for coverage. Plot the estimates against window length at fixed sample count and identify the interval where both errors are small. Move the exponent closer to one and repeat; the useful window typically shifts because the physical edge carries a larger fraction of the signal.

For a more expensive black-box integrand, retain samples when halving the transformed step at fixed cutoff. All old abscissae lie on the finer grid. The trapezoid at the finer step is the sum of half the old approximation and the new midpoint samples times the finer spacing. This reuse affects computational cost, not the mathematics of the tail correction. It supports two separate reports: how much the estimated integral changed and how many genuinely new evaluations were required to obtain that change.

A local OneSimulation comparison in the notebook uses twenty-four line elements on the retained window. Eight mapped Gaussian points per element sample the transformed density at different locations from the trapezoid. Compare that sum with the analytical finite-window integral. Agreement here isolates the numerical integration on the interval. The previously calculated tails must still be included when reporting the complete physical signal.

Tutorial on endpoint mass and stopping criteria

1. An instrument cannot resolve the first millionth of the width. For \(\alpha=2/3\), what fraction of the total signal would be omitted if this strip were simply discarded?

Work through the solution

The fraction is \(\varepsilon^{1-\alpha}\). Substituting \(10^{-6}\) and \(1/3\) gives \(10^{-2}\), or one per cent. The full integral is three, so the omitted integral is 0.03 in the dimensionless units of the model. This error would persist even for an exact integration over the resolved remainder.

2. With \(\alpha=1/2\), compare the first-panel midpoint contribution to its exact value. What follows for the number of uniform panels needed merely to halve this contribution to the error?

Work through the solution

The exact value is \(2\sqrt h\), and the midpoint gives \(\sqrt{2h}\). Their difference is \((2-\sqrt2)\sqrt h\). To halve it one must quarter \(h\), hence quadruple the number of panels on the fixed overall interval. A second-order rule for a smooth function would suggest a much smaller increase.

3. Two calculations with \(T=2\) agree to ten printed digits. Does this suffice to report ten digits in the full integral? Propose an additional diagnostic for a density without a known antiderivative.

Work through the solution

Agreement supports resolution inside the selected window. Extend both tails at comparable step and record the changes from the new bands separately. Inspect their decay and, if the model supplies an asymptotic bound, integrate that bound beyond the last sampled band. Almost equal numbers at one truncation do not constrain a contribution that neither run sampled.

Further reading · Masatake Mori, discovery and development of the double-exponential transformation

Problem sheet 7 · Project · How many sensors measure the mean around a ring?

Design, challenge and repair a quadrature for periodic temperature measurements. Distinguish a sampled mean, reconstruction of a continuous profile and error in a reference calculation.

Predict three instrument readings

A manufactured test field is \(T(\theta)=20+2\cos(5\theta)+0.8\cos(12\theta)\) °C. Determine its exact spatial mean. Predict measurements from rings with 5, 6 and 7 equally spaced sensors at zero offset. Calculate their mean using a finite geometric sum.

Work through the solution

The continuous mean is 20 °C because both cosines integrate to zero over a full turn. For mode \(k\) set \(z=e^{2\pi i k/N}\). When \(z\ne1\), the discrete average is \((1-z^N)/[N(1-z)]=0\). When \(z=1\), every term is one. Taking real parts leaves only harmonics divisible by the sensor count. Five sensors see a mean of 22, six give 20.8 and seven give 20. These results describe three different instruments observing exactly the same field. A 12-sensor instrument also measures 20.8, so agreement with a coarser grid can be misleading.

Build a diagnostic from a second run

Turn the six-sensor ring by 15°. Determine which part of the signal changes in its mean. Can the average of the two runs recover the continuous mean? Test the 12th harmonic with the rotatable sensor ring. Its test harmonic has amplitude 3 °C. Compare the sign reversal and rescale its mean offset by 0.8/3 to match this instrument.

Work through the solution

The twelfth harmonic changes sign since \(12(15^\circ)=180^\circ\). The fifth harmonic still averages to zero. The second mean is 19.2 °C; the two-run average is 20. The offset is a quarter of the old sample interval. A half-interval rotation of 30° would bring the twelfth harmonic through a full cycle and keep the erroneous mean. Choose offsets using the alias you intend to diagnose.

Use an interval integral for an independent calibration

A second sensor samples a reference profile \(u(x)=x^4\) along a straight rail with scaled coordinate \(0\le x\le1\). On one, two and four equal panels its trapezoidal values are 0.5, 0.28125 and 0.220703125. Build the first two extrapolation columns. Then change the exponent to one half in the extrapolation experiment. Explain where using the wrong error power would distort the calibration.

Work through the solution

Two first extrapolates are \((4\times0.28125-0.5)/3=0.2083333\) and \((4\times0.220703125-0.28125)/3=0.20052083\). Combining them with denominator 15 gives 0.2. The square-root profile lacks a bounded derivative at the endpoint, so a full even-power error expansion cannot be assumed. The observed convergence ratio is the diagnostic in this run.

State what the specification guarantees

The design must integrate any trigonometric polynomial with modes through 12 and also reconstruct it uniquely if asked. Find sufficient uniform sensor counts for each task and explain their difference.

Work through the solution

A 13-point ring integrates all these modes exactly because none of \(1,\ldots,12\) is divisible by 13. To recover all sine and cosine coefficients through mode 12 one needs \(2(12)+1=25\) real degrees of freedom. A 25-point uniform ring suffices. The mean is a single linear functional and can be identifiable when the full field is not.

Problem sheet 8 · Integrate a rapidly oscillating signal without resolving every crest

A lock-in detector measures the in-phase integral \(I(k)=\int_0^1 f(x)\cos(kx)\,dx\). The carrier frequency is known exactly from the instrument. The envelope is smooth and expensive to evaluate. Design a quadrature rule that spends those evaluations on the envelope, and find a case where even the improved method gives a deceptively small answer.

Predict a complete alias on a coarse scan

Let \(f(x)=1+x^2\) and \(k=16\pi\). Use eight uniform panels and include both endpoints with half weight in the trapezoidal rule. What does the instrument predict, and what is the true integral?

Work through the solution

The carrier completes exactly one full cycle per panel. Its values at every sampled point are therefore one, so the naive sum integrates only the envelope. It gives \(1+1/3+1/(6\cdot8^2)=1.3359375\). Integrating the oscillatory expression by parts twice gives \(I(k)=2\sin k/k+2\cos k/k^2-2\sin k/k^3\). At \(16\pi\) the true result is \(2/(16\pi)^2=0.00079157\). The small true signal comes from cancellation of lobes with slightly unequal envelope weights. Nothing in the sampled carrier values reveals those lobes. A small residual between the trapezoidal estimates of a few adjacent sampled segments would be uninformative here.

Record which quantities are known before choosing a rule. If the underlying carrier were unknown and only sampled instrument voltages were available, this example would be an information problem: the samples are also consistent with an unmodulated envelope. Here the oscillator frequency and phase are controlled and available between sample positions. A numerical method can exploit that extra knowledge without evaluating the expensive part of the integrand any more frequently.

Integrate a linear envelope against the exact carrier

Approximate only the envelope on a panel of width \(h\) and midpoint \(m\) by \(a+bt\), where \(t=x-m\). Find \(a,b\) from endpoint samples and derive the exact panel integral of this approximation.

Work through the solution

Use \(a=(f_L+f_R)/2\), \(b=(f_R-f_L)/h\), and set \(d=kh/2\). Expand \(\cos[k(m+t)]\) into cosine and sine products. Odd integrands integrate to zero on the symmetric interval. The constant part gives \(2a\cos(km)\sin d/k\). The slope term gives \(-2b\sin(km)(\sin d-d\cos d)/k^2\), after an integration by parts of \(t\sin(kt)\). Add the two terms and sum over panels. This is a piecewise-linear member of the Filon family: interpolate a slowly varying amplitude and compute its oscillatory moments. It integrates every globally linear envelope exactly for every carrier frequency, up to roundoff.

The individual moment expressions require care when \(d\) is very small. In particular \(\sin d-d\cos d=d^3/3-d^5/30+\cdots\); evaluating it by direct subtraction near zero loses significant digits. In the limit \(k\to0\) the constant moment becomes \(h\), the odd contribution vanishes, and the rule reduces to an ordinary trapezoid for the envelope. For high frequency the same formula behaves quite differently from sampling the product. The analytic moments retain the signs and magnitudes of oscillatory contributions between the nodes.

Explain an apparently perfect zero and recover curvature

Return to eight complete periods on eight panels. The fitted linear rule now gives zero. Is this answer exact? What new measurement can recover the missing signal?

Work through the solution

Here \(d=\pi\) and \(\sin(km)=0\) on every panel. Both moment contributions vanish separately, regardless of the endpoint amplitudes. The constant and linear parts of each local envelope make no net contribution over a centred complete cosine period aligned this way. The exact quadratic curvature does contribute. Introduce one midpoint envelope measurement and fit a quadratic through the endpoints and midpoint; its exact oscillatory second moment recovers the polynomial test identically. On these aligned panels a simpler check is to compute the residual \(f(x)-p_1(x)=(x-x_L)(x-x_R)\). The integral of its quadratic term supplies exactly the signal discarded by the linear rule. The linear answer is far closer in absolute terms than the aliased trapezoid, yet its relative error for the small target is still one hundred percent.

Dimensional reasoning makes the restoration plausible. For a twice differentiable amplitude the pointwise error of linear interpolation on one panel is bounded by \(\max|f''|h^2/8\). Multiplying by \(|\cos|\le1\) and integrating across all panels yields a uniform absolute error bound of the same order on this unit interval. It says little about relative error when the exact integral is a small difference of much larger signed contributions. An acceptance criterion for a detector with a known noise floor should therefore include an absolute tolerance expressed in the actual output units.

Test resolution and report a useful tolerance

Vary the number of panels and sweep the carrier through adjacent frequencies in the live graph. Which measurements distinguish genuinely small coherent output from values produced by aliasing?

Work through the solution

Compute an independent reference by integrating the polynomial test analytically. Compare absolute errors on both sides of an integer number of cycles, including the exact alignment at eight cycles. The aliased trapezoid produces peaks when sampled phases line up. The Filon result follows the reference more closely once the envelope is resolved, with the special zero at the exactly aligned coarse case explained above. Repeat after doubling panel count and changing the start phase in an actual experiment. The true in-phase projection also changes with phase, so compare against the corresponding analytic reference for that phase instead of demanding agreement between physically different projections.

The graph displays integrals versus the number of carrier cycles, holding the number of envelope panels fixed over the sweep. Start with eight panels and include the neighbourhood of eight cycles. Then increase the panel count to sixteen. Read the signed reference and the two calculated estimates at the selected frequency. A rapidly oscillating but known carrier is a structural clue: a rule using its exact moments and an independently tested envelope approximation can avoid catastrophic sampling aliases.
