How a turning point supplies the missing semiclassical phase

Quantum states, dynamics and measurement

Far from a turning point the wavelength follows the classical momentum. At the turning point that approximation becomes singular. Derive the Airy connection, quantise a smooth well, distinguish a hard wall and estimate a tunnelling exponent with a defensible error check.

Expand the action before interpreting a local wavelength

For a stationary one-dimensional state with smooth potential \(V(x)\), write \(\psi=e^{iS(x)/\hbar}\). Substitution into Schrödinger's equation gives \(S'^2-i\hbar S''=2m(E-V)=p^2(x)\). Expand \(S=S_0+(\hbar/i)S_1+\cdots\). The leading terms give \(S_0'=\pm p\) and \(S_1'=-p'/(2p)\) in an allowed region where \(p>0\). Thus travelling waves have form \(C p^{-1/2}\exp[\pm i\int^x p\,dx/\hbar]\). The amplitude factor is physically essential: for a single such branch the current to leading order is \(\pm|C|^2/m\), independent of \(x\), as required for steady flux in a real potential. A constant amplitude with variable wavenumber would predict current piling up in a stationary region.

A local condition for this expansion is \(\hbar|p'|/p^2\ll1\) together with control over higher derivatives on the wavelength scale. Away from a barrier the phase can remain accurate even if absolute amplitude normalisation must be fixed globally. Where \(E<V\), define \(\kappa=\sqrt{2m(V-E)}\) with units of momentum. The local solutions become \(\kappa^{-1/2}\exp[\pm\int\kappa\,dx/\hbar]\). A single real decaying branch carries zero probability current by itself. Stationary tunnelling flux through a finite barrier involves a complex combination satisfying both exterior matching conditions, even though the leading size of transmission can often be inferred from the decaying exponential.

Resolve the apparent divergence near a simple turning point

Let \(x_t\) satisfy \(V(x_t)=E\), with slope \(F=V'(x_t)>0\). Linearise \(V-E\simeq F(x-x_t)\). The local Schrödinger equation becomes \(\psi''=(2mF/\hbar^2)(x-x_t)\psi\). Introduce length \(\ell=(\hbar^2/(2mF))^{1/3}\) and \(\xi=(x-x_t)/\ell\), leaving \(d^2\psi/d\xi^2=\xi\psi\). Its two independent solutions are \(\operatorname{Ai}\) and \(\operatorname{Bi}\). On the positive side \(\operatorname{Ai}(\xi)\) decays, whereas \(\operatorname{Bi}(\xi)\) grows. A bound state extending into a semi-infinite forbidden region selects the former. At \(\xi=0\) the Airy function is finite; the apparent divergence \(p^{-1/2}\) came from extending the outer approximation beyond its range.

The asymptotic forms for \(s>0\) large are

\[\operatorname{Ai}(s)\sim\frac{e^{-2s^{3/2}/3}}{2\sqrt\pi\,s^{1/4}},\]
\[\operatorname{Ai}(-s)\sim\frac{\sin(2s^{3/2}/3+\pi/4)}{\sqrt\pi\,s^{1/4}} .\]

For a linear slope, \(2s^{3/2}/3\) equals \(|\int_{x_t}^x p\,dx|/\hbar\) on the allowed side or \(\int_{x_t}^x\kappa\,dx/\hbar\) on the forbidden side. Matching in an interval where \(|x-x_t|\gg\ell\) but the potential remains effectively linear gives the connection from a decaying tail of coefficient \(C\) to \(2C\,p^{-1/2}\sin[\hbar^{-1}\int_x^{x_t}p\,dx+\pi/4]\). The coefficient two and phase \(\pi/4\) both come from the same local solution. A turning point with zero slope, a sharp step or two turning points closer than their Airy lengths requires a different local treatment.

Join two decaying tails to a standing wave

For a smooth well with two well-separated simple turning points \(x_1,x_2\), the left tail matches to \(\sin[\hbar^{-1}\int_{x_1}^xp\,dx+\pi/4]\). The right tail matches to \(\sin[\hbar^{-1}\int_x^{x_2}p\,dx+\pi/4]\). Their zeros and logarithmic derivatives must coincide throughout the overlap, up to an overall sign. The sum of the two phase arguments is the full one-way action divided by \(\hbar\) plus \(\pi/2\), which must be an integer multiple of \(\pi\). Taking \(n\ge0\) gives

\[\int_{x_1}^{x_2}p(x,E_n)\,dx=\pi\hbar(n+1/2).\]

For a harmonic oscillator the turning point magnitude is \(a=\sqrt{2E/(m\omega^2)}\) and \(p=m\omega\sqrt{a^2-x^2}\). The action is the area of a semicircle times \(m\omega\), equal to \(\pi E/\omega\). The rule reproduces \(E_n=\hbar\omega(n+1/2)\) exactly for this special spectrum, even though its outer WKB wavefunction is still inaccurate near each turning point and globally for low quantum number. Equality of a spectral result does not imply equality of spatial densities or agreement of all observables.

Different boundaries change the phase budget. Take a hard wall at \(x=0\) and a linearly rising potential \(Fx\) for \(x>0\). The solution decaying to the right is \(\operatorname{Ai}[(x-E/F)/\ell]\). Its exact energies follow from \(\operatorname{Ai}(-E/F\ell)=0\). The wall imposes a node without an Airy phase at the left. Setting the right-matched standing wave to zero at the wall gives \(\int_0^{E/F}p\,dx=\pi\hbar(n+3/4)\). Thus \(E/(F\ell)\simeq[3\pi(n+3/4)/2]^{2/3}\). For \(n=0\) this is about 2.320 versus the first exact zero 2.338; with \(n=4\) the approximation is about 7.94. Replacing the wall by a second smooth turning point would instead introduce an erroneous half offset.

Separate a credible exponential estimate from its prefactor

For a barrier with two simple turning points \(x_a,x_b\), a large forbidden action \(I=\int_{x_a}^{x_b}\kappa\,dx/\hbar\) suppresses the transmitted flux approximately as \(T\simeq e^{-2I}\), up to prefactors and corrections that depend on geometry and matching. For a triangular forbidden region with height \(V_0-E=\Delta\) at \(x=0\) falling as \(\Delta-Fx\) to zero at \(\Delta/F\), its action is \(I=2\sqrt{2m}\Delta^{3/2}/(3\hbar F)\). A discontinuous incident-side boundary may affect the prefactor substantially. It does not justify assigning a second smooth-turning-point phase to the discontinuity. For a rectangular barrier the exact thick-barrier prefactor is \(16E(V_0-E)/V_0^2\) times the decaying exponential at equal exterior potentials. It can exceed one while total transmission remains small.

Suppose two measurements change only the forbidden width of a flat barrier from \(d\) to \(d+\Delta d\), keeping both interfaces and asymptotic energies the same. In the thick limit the ratio \(T(d+\Delta d)/T(d)\simeq e^{-2\kappa\Delta d/\hbar}\) cancels the common prefactor. If \(\kappa/\hbar=2.0\,\mathrm{nm^{-1}}\) and \(\Delta d=0.50\) nm, this ratio is \(e^{-2}=0.1353\). An offset error in barrier width that leaves this ratio unchanged can still cause a large error in absolute transmission. Near the top two turning points can merge, \(I\) becomes small, and the raw exponential lacks a unitary connection to the over-barrier reflection regime. Use a suitable uniform approximation or a direct scattering solution there.

Predict a high-energy spectrum without a closed-form wavefunction

Consider a pure quartic trap \(V(x)=\lambda x^4\) with \(\lambda>0\). Classical turning points lie at \(x=\pm a,\ a=(E/\lambda)^{1/4}\). Unlike the harmonic oscillator its period changes with energy, so its levels cannot be equally spaced. The semiclassical action can be evaluated once in dimensionless coordinates and then rescaled to any \(m,\lambda,E\):

\[A(E)=\int_{-a}^a\sqrt{2m(E-\lambda x^4)}\,dx=2\sqrt{2m}\,\lambda^{-1/4}E^{3/4} I_4,\]
\[I_4=\int_0^1\sqrt{1-y^4}\,dy .\]

With \(s=y^4\), \(I_4=\frac14B(1/4,3/2)\simeq0.8740\). Quantising \(A(E_n)=\pi\hbar(n+1/2)\) predicts \(E_n\simeq[\pi\hbar(n+1/2)\lambda^{1/4}/(2\sqrt{2m}I_4)]^{4/3}\). The dependence \(E\propto n^{4/3}\) and the dimensional factor \(\hbar^{4/3}\lambda^{1/3}m^{-2/3}\) can be checked independently from scaling the full Schrödinger equation. That dimensional argument does not supply the coefficient or the half-integer offset. The action and boundary phases supply the additional physics.

Choose units such that \(H=-d^2/dx^2+x^4\). Then \(2m=\hbar=1\) in the scaled equation, and the leading energies are \([\pi(n+1/2)/(2I_4)]^{4/3}\). For \(n=0\) this gives about 0.868, below the accurately computed ground value around 1.060. An independent variational Gaussian \(\psi=(\beta/\pi)^{1/4}e^{-\beta x^2/2}\) has energy \(\beta/2+3/(4\beta^2)\), minimised at \(\beta^3=3\) and giving 1.082. Its proximity to the true ground energy has a different status: the Gaussian energy is an upper bound by the variational principle, while leading WKB provides no universal bound for this potential. Using agreement between two approximate numbers as proof of a rigorous interval would require actual lower and upper estimates.

At larger \(n\) the period relation provides an additional prediction. Since \(A\propto E^{3/4}\), \(T_{\rm cl}=2\,dA/dE\propto E^{-1/4}\); the frequency increases at larger amplitude, and the local spacing should grow as \(E^{1/4}\). Numerically compute a handful of high levels with a direct finite-difference or spectral Hamiltonian, then plot \(E_n^{3/4}\) against \(n+1/2\). A nearly linear trend is a more sensitive diagnostic of the predicted law than comparing a single apparent power from a log plot at low \(n\). Increase domain size and basis cutoff independently. A truncation that confines high-energy tails can falsely steepen the spacing, while a coarse grid can flatten the kinetic-energy dispersion. Both errors become more visible for rapidly oscillating states.

Relate a barrier action to a coherent population oscillation

Take two identical minima separated by a smooth barrier. At energies below the barrier top but above each isolated-well ground, there are two classically allowed intervals and a central forbidden region. If the barrier is sufficiently opaque, construct approximate localised states \(|L\rangle,|R\rangle\) with equal one-well energy \(E_0\). In their span the simplest symmetric Hamiltonian is \(H=E_0 I-J(|L\rangle\langle R|+|R\rangle\langle L|)\) with \(J>0\) in a suitable phase convention. The even and odd combinations have energies \(E_0-J,E_0+J\). The tunnel splitting is \(2J\), set to leading exponential order by the overlap of amplitudes through the central forbidden interval.

A single under-barrier wavefunction amplitude carries \(e^{-I}\), \(I=\int\kappa dx/\hbar\). For an open barrier the transmission probability is proportional to \(e^{-2I}\). For a symmetric double-well splitting the relevant quantity is a coherent off-diagonal energy amplitude, so its leading exponential is \(J\propto e^{-I}\), times a prefactor involving the oscillation scales and matching in the two wells. Confusing these exponents can lead to a severe error when using a calculated spatial barrier to predict a coherent tunnel frequency. The exact prefactor depends on the potential and turning-point normalisations. A direct symmetric numerical eigenproblem giving the difference of two nearly equal energies requires enough precision to resolve an exponentially small splitting.

Prepare \(|L\rangle\) at time zero. Its evolution under the two-state Hamiltonian gives \(P_R(t)=\sin^2(Jt/\hbar)\); the first full transfer time is \(\pi\hbar/(2J)\). If the observed energy splitting expressed as ordinary frequency is \(2J/h=20\) Hz, the first transfer occurs at 25 ms. Changing only the barrier action by \(+0.70\) at fixed prefactor multiplies the splitting by \(e^{-0.70}=0.497\) and approximately doubles the transfer time. An open-barrier transmission experiment over the same change instead sees factor \(e^{-1.4}=0.247\). Both tests depend exponentially on barrier shape but interrogate different quantities.

Now add a calibrated tilt \(\epsilon=E_L-E_R\). The \(2\times2\) Hamiltonian after trace subtraction is \((\epsilon/2)\sigma_z-J\sigma_x\), and the maximum transfer falls to \(4J^2/(\epsilon^2+4J^2)\). At \(\epsilon=2J\) it is one half while the oscillation angular frequency increases from \(2J/\hbar\) to \(2\sqrt2J/\hbar\). A loss of transfer contrast need not be environmental decoherence; static tilt alone yields this combination of increased frequency and reduced amplitude. Measuring both quantities and reversing the tilt tests the two-level model. If a higher intrawell level lies within the tilt or excitation range, a single tunnelling doublet no longer spans the relevant motion and a larger basis is required.

Understand the angular correction at a radial turning point

In three dimensions the reduced radial equation on \(r>0\) has a centrifugal term \(\hbar^2l(l+1)/(2mr^2)\). Applying the flat-coordinate WKB rule directly to this equation can give incorrect phases at its singular endpoint, especially for low \(l\). A way to see the appropriate angular correction without guessing is to set \(r=r_0 e^x\), mapping the half-line to all real \(x\), and write \(u(r)=e^{x/2}w(x)\). The arbitrary reference \(r_0\) keeps the logarithm dimensionless. Since \(d/dr=r^{-1}d/dx\), differentiating twice yields

\[\frac{d^2u}{dr^2}=\frac{e^{x/2}}{r^2}\left(w''-\frac14 w\right).\]

The resulting equation for \(w\) has squared local action momentum in \(x\) proportional to \(2mr^2(E-V)-\hbar^2(l+1/2)^2\). Thus returning its action \(\int p_xdx\) to \(r\) uses \(l(l+1)\to(l+1/2)^2\) in the radial semiclassical momentum. This is the Langer correction. It does not change the exact angular eigenvalue \(L^2=\hbar^2l(l+1)\); it accounts for the coordinate transformation and endpoint behaviour of the semiclassical representation. Confusing those statements would alter the exact radial equation to repair an approximate integration rule.

An instructive check is the isotropic oscillator. For \(V=m\omega^2r^2/2\) and effective classical angular momentum \(L=(l+1/2)\hbar\), the radial action over its two roots is \(A_r=(\pi/2)(E/\omega-L)\). One can obtain this without an explicit contour integral by noting that radial motion repeats at twice the Cartesian frequency, so \(dA_r/dE=\pi/(2\omega)\). Fix the integration constant at the circular-orbit threshold \(E=\omega L\), where the two radial turning points meet and \(A_r=0\). Quantising \(A_r=\pi\hbar(n_r+1/2)\) gives \(E=\hbar\omega(2n_r+l+3/2)\), matching the exact three-dimensional oscillator spectrum. For \(l=0\) the naive uncorrected centrifugal term has no inner turning point; transferring a generic two-smooth-turning-point formula into that half-line problem without reconsidering its origin is especially unsafe.

Calculate an exponentially small transmission without subtracting nearly equal numbers

In a classically forbidden interval the local solution contains a growing and a decaying branch. In a long interval numerical transfer matrices can contain elements as large as \(e^I\), while the quantity sought is as small as \(e^{-2I}\). Straight propagation of a decaying boundary condition in the unstable direction can contaminate it with a tiny growing roundoff component which eventually dominates. A plot matching the expected slope over the first few decay lengths gives no guarantee that a final tiny flux is accurate. Numerically stable alternatives include propagating a logarithmic derivative with controlled handling of its poles, matching independently normalised left and right solutions, or composing scattering matrices for short slices while retaining decaying propagation factors.

For a square barrier the exact transmission at \(E<V_0\) in equal exterior regions is \(T=[1+V_0^2\sinh^2(\kappa d/\hbar)/(4E(V_0-E))]^{-1}\). With \(E=V_0/2\) this simplifies to \(\operatorname{sech}^2 I\). At \(I=20\) its value is approximately \(4e^{-40}=1.70\times10^{-17}\). Computing \(T=1-R\) in ordinary double precision can give exactly zero since the reflection probability rounds to one. Compute transmitted amplitude or its logarithm directly and compare \(\log T\simeq\log4-2I\). At a moderate \(I\), where both currents are accurately representable, independently verify \(R+T=1\). Moving the benchmarking point across the barrier-opacity range tests both the physical approximation and the floating-point representation of the desired observable.

Resolve a wave through a classical turning point

Vary the physical length scale in a locally linear potential. Compare the numerical Airy solution and the two semiclassical branches near and away from the turn.

Open the live experiment

Connect semiclassical action to counting states in phase space

The action quantisation rule can also be read as a statement about the number of energy levels below a chosen energy. In a one-dimensional smooth confining well the classically accessible region in \((x,p)\) has area \(\Omega(E)=\int dx\int_{-p(x)}^{p(x)}dp=2A(E)\). Roughly one stationary state fits per phase-space area \(2\pi\hbar\), with an endpoint correction from the turning-point phases. The smoothed density of states follows by differentiating \(\bar N(E)\simeq\Omega(E)/(2\pi\hbar)\), giving \(\bar\rho(E)=T_{\rm cl}/(2\pi\hbar)\). This counts levels after smoothing over individual spacing; the exact count is a staircase and its derivative a sum of delta functions. A formula for a smooth density cannot predict each state in a near-degenerate doublet without additional phase and tunnelling information.

For a particle in a two-dimensional rigid rectangular region, apply the same counting idea in four-dimensional phase space. With area \(\mathcal A=L_xL_y\), accessible momentum disk \(p_x^2+p_y^2<2mE\), the scalar smoothed count is \(\bar N(E)\simeq\mathcal A\pi(2mE)/(2\pi\hbar)^2=\mathcal A mE/(2\pi\hbar^2)\). Its derivative is a constant, unlike the \(E^{-1/2}\) free one-dimensional density and the \(\sqrt E\) three-dimensional density. This leading area law can be checked by directly counting positive integer lattice points satisfying \((n_x\pi/L_x)^2+(n_y\pi/L_y)^2<k^2\) in a Dirichlet rectangle. It works after smoothing when many modes fit in the domain and makes no assumption that the rectangular shape itself models all possible billiards.

A more sensitive check accounts for the excluded axes in that first-quadrant lattice count. For a Dirichlet rectangle subtract half of the positive-axis contributions at each boundary of the quadrant. In terms of perimeter \(\mathcal P=2(L_x+L_y)\), the resulting first two terms are \(\bar N(k)\simeq\mathcal A k^2/(4\pi)-\mathcal P k/(4\pi)+O(1)\) after appropriate smoothing, with fluctuations from discrete modes beyond this local expansion. Neumann boundaries reverse the sign of the perimeter term because modes constant along one coordinate are admitted. Area alone determines the bulk growth, whereas boundary condition affects the next correction. The finite corner term and spectral fluctuations require more care than assigning one universal integer offset to every billiard.

The distinction between smoothing and exact prediction also explains why counting every computed eigenvalue only up to an arbitrarily sharp threshold can produce apparent convergence jumps as the mesh improves. If a true eigenvalue lies near the threshold, a small numerical shift changes an integer count by one. Compare a range of cumulative counts, track nearby eigenvalue residuals and study the smoothed density with a bandwidth large enough to include several levels but small compared with the energy scale of interest. Repeating for two geometries of equal area and different perimeter tests actual geometric information beyond an overall energy scale.

Measure the boundary correction in a square cavity

A scalar mode calculation uses a square of side \(10.0\) length units and reports all Dirichlet modes with \(k<5.00\) inverse length units. Find the leading area estimate, add the perimeter correction, and compare with a direct lattice-point count.

Derive and check the result

The area contribution is \(100(25)/(4\pi)=198.94\); the perimeter subtraction is \(40(5)/(4\pi)=15.92\), giving a smoothed estimate \(183.03\) before corner and oscillatory terms. The exact count uses positive integers \(n_x,n_y\) satisfying \(n_x^2+n_y^2<(50/\pi)^2\simeq253.30\). For each \(n_x=1,\ldots,15\), count \(n_y\le\lfloor\sqrt{253.30-n_x^2}\rfloor\) and sum. The values are \(15,15,15,15,15,14,14,13,13,12,11,10,9,7,5\), giving 183. Agreement at this threshold is close, while the leading area term alone is about sixteen high. Nearby thresholds can have different residual fluctuations, so the close integer agreement at this one wave number should not be treated as an exact two-term counting rule. The same calculation on a different aspect ratio at fixed area would change both the perimeter and the lattice pattern.

Measure a reflection phase separately from the travel action

In a one-dimensional cavity a quantisation condition can be written as a round-trip phase budget. Let an allowed region accumulate one-way phase \(\theta(E)\) and let reflections at its left and right bounding regions have phase shifts \(\phi_L(E),\phi_R(E)\) for unit-modulus reflection. A self-reproducing wave requires \(2\theta(E)+\phi_L(E)+\phi_R(E)=2\pi n\), with an integer depending on the chosen phase origin. A rigid Dirichlet wall reflects with amplitude \(-1\); a smooth decaying turning point contributes a different reflection phase that the Airy matching fixed. Adding \(2\pi\) to one reflection phase simply relabels the integer, but an energy-dependent boundary phase alters the spacing and cannot be removed by a constant relabelling.

A useful boundary that can be solved without approximation is a finite step \(V=0\) for \(x<0,\ V=V_0>E\) for \(x>0\). With incident wave \(e^{ikx}\), reflected amplitude \(r e^{-ikx}\) and exterior evanescent \(t e^{-\kappa x}\), where \(k,\kappa\) here are inverse lengths, continuity of the value and derivative gives \(1+r=t,\ ik(1-r)=-\kappa t\). Solving yields \(r=(k-i\kappa)/(k+i\kappa)\). Its magnitude is one and its phase can be written \(-2\arctan(\kappa/k)\). As \(V_0\) tends to infinity with \(E\) fixed, the phase tends to \(-\pi\), giving the rigid-wall limit. Just below the step threshold it approaches zero. Two mirrors with equally complete reflection can therefore produce different cavity eigenfrequencies.

Near the rigid-wall limit expand \(r\simeq-e^{2ik/\kappa}\). To leading order the exterior behaves like a rigid reflecting plane displaced a penetration length \(1/\kappa\) beyond the interface. For a deep symmetric finite well of width \(L\), the phase condition approximately replaces \(L\) by \(L+2/\kappa(E)\). This shift lowers levels relative to a rigid well of the same geometric width. It is only an approximation because penetration depends on energy. Matching logarithmic derivatives independently on the two sides is preferable near threshold or when the barrier shape varies over the penetration length.

Infer a boundary penetration from a measured free spectral interval

A long one-dimensional cavity with identical deep finite barriers has a measured low-mode wavenumber interval 2% smaller than \(\pi/L\). Under the constant penetration approximation infer \(1/\kappa\) relative to \(L\) and identify a check on the approximation.

Derive and check the result

Set \(\pi/(L+2/\kappa)=0.98\pi/L\). Then \(2/\kappa L=1/0.98-1=0.0204\), or a penetration distance \(1/\kappa=0.0102L\) at each wall. For \(L=100\) nm this is about 1.02 nm. Measure several successive intervals to see whether one penetration length describes them. An increasing penetration with energy changes the reflection-phase derivative and therefore the spacing; the physical group delay then contains travel time in the allowed region plus boundary delay. A geometrically longer rigid cavity would produce a similar single interval but a different energy dependence, so an isolated observation does not uniquely identify the cause.

Tutorial · determine what a semiclassical estimate can certify

Recover the period and level spacing from an action curve

For \(A(E)=\int_{x_1}^{x_2}p\,dx\) in a smooth bound well, derive \(dA/dE\) and infer the local energy spacing. Apply it to a 100 kHz oscillator and express spacing in both joules and electronvolts.

Work through the solution

The endpoint derivative terms vanish because \(p=0\) at both ends. Inside, \(\partial p/\partial E=m/p=1/v\), so \(dA/dE\) equals the classical one-way travel time \(T_{\rm cl}/2\). From the quantisation rule increasing \(n\) by one gives local spacing \(\Delta E\simeq2\pi\hbar/T_{\rm cl}=hf_{\rm cl}\). At 100 kHz this is \(6.626\times10^{-29}\) J or \(4.136\times10^{-10}\) eV. In an anharmonic potential the classical period generally depends on energy; use the local slope or integrate between adjacent solutions rather than assuming a universal ladder spacing.

Check whether the Airy overlap interval exists

For a model atom with mass \(1.44\times10^{-25}\) kg let \(F=1.0\times10^{-21}\) N at the turning point. Find the Airy length. If \(|V''|=1.0\times10^{-16}\,\mathrm{N/m}\), estimate the relative quadratic correction at \(5\ell\).

Work through the solution

The length is \((\hbar^2/2mF)^{1/3}\simeq3.38\times10^{-8}\) m. The ratio of quadratic potential term to linear term at \(5\ell\) is \(|V''|5\ell/(2F)\simeq0.00845\). Thus several Airy lengths can fit in a region with small curvature correction for these numbers. The parameter \(|x-x_t|/\ell=5\) should still be checked against the desired asymptotic precision; the result supports an overlap interval but does not guarantee a prescribed decimal accuracy. Bringing a second turning point within a few such lengths changes the matching problem qualitatively.

Compare a triangular action with a rectangular shortcut

A linearly falling barrier has initial forbidden wavenumber \(\kappa_0/\hbar=3.0\,\mathrm{nm^{-1}}\) and width \(d=2.0\) nm. Compare its exponential estimate with that of a rectangle using initial height throughout. What would a rectangle at the average potential height predict?

Work through the solution

For the triangle, \(\kappa(x)=\kappa_0\sqrt{1-x/d}\), so \(I=(2/3)(\kappa_0/\hbar)d=4.0\), giving \(e^{-8}=3.35\times10^{-4}\). The initial-height rectangle gives \(I=6\) or \(e^{-12}=6.14\times10^{-6}\). Averaging the potential height first gives wavenumber \(\kappa_0/\sqrt2\), \(I=4.24\) and \(e^{-8.49}\simeq2.06\times10^{-4}\). The action averages the square root of the local energy gap, not the gap itself. Agreement in mean barrier height is insufficient for predicting an exponential observable. Exact matching or a direct numerical probability current would be needed to determine the prefactor.

Further reading

Measure gravity with a pendulum of finite amplitude

Mechanics in the laboratory

A metre-long pendulum is an appealing clock because its small-angle period depends only on length and gravity. A precise clock can nevertheless give a precisely wrong value of gravity. Derive the full period, trace the systematic error to an identifiable approximation and design a measurement that distinguishes a length error from a release-angle error.

Define the measured length and time

Use a point bob on a massless, inextensible string attached to a fixed pivot. Let \(L\) be the distance from pivot to the bob's centre of mass, and \(\theta\) the signed departure from the downward vertical. For a rigid extended bob, rotational inertia about its own centre adds to \(mL^2\); a length measured to the bottom of the bob is neither of these dynamical lengths. Measure the elapsed time between successive crossings of a photogate in the same direction. Crossings in opposite directions give half of the period in a symmetric oscillation. A stationary turning point is a poor timing marker because the position error there produces a large time error.

At rest at the release angle \(\theta_0\), the energy is \(mgL(1-\cos\theta_0)\). At a later angle the speed is \(L|\dot\theta|\). Equating kinetic plus potential energy to the release value gives \(\dot\theta^2=(2g/L)(\cos\theta-\cos\theta_0)\). Only a quarter cycle is needed. Inverting the angular speed and integrating from zero to \(\theta_0\) produces a square-root singularity at release. The period is finite since integrating the inverse square root over a small interval gives a square root.

Resolve the turning point before approximating

Write \(k=\sin(\theta_0/2)\) and substitute \(\sin(\theta/2)=k\sin u\). The vanishing square root cancels a factor \(\cos u\) from the differential. One obtains the complete elliptic integral of the first kind with modulus \(k\).

\[T=4\sqrt{\frac Lg}\,K(k),\]
\[K(k)=\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{du}{\sqrt{1-k^2\sin^2u}}.\]

Expanding its integrand yields \(K(k)=(\pi/2)(1+k^2/4+9k^4/64+\cdots)\). Since \(k\simeq\theta_0/2\) at small amplitude, the first fractional period correction is \(\theta_0^2/16\) with angle in radians. It is positive. At larger amplitude the tangential component of gravity rises more slowly with angle than the linear restoring law predicts. The pendulum spends longer near the turns. Passing degrees directly into the quadratic correction would give nonsense even if the timer and recorded angle were perfect.

Infer the bias in gravity

Suppose the true acceleration is \(9.81\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}\), the length is 1.000 m and the release angle is 30 degrees. The small-angle period is about 2.0061 s and the finite-amplitude period about 2.0410 s. Interpreting the longer measured period by the linear formula would give about \(9.48\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}\). The fractional gravity error is about twice the fractional period error with opposite sign because gravity is inferred from \(T^{-2}\). Timing a hundred periods reduces random timing error but leaves this systematic shift.

The numerical curve evaluates the complete elliptic integral. It uses the arithmetic–geometric mean identity \(K(k)=\pi/[2\,\mathrm{AGM}(1,\sqrt{1-k^2})]\), so no sampled point sits on the turning point. The single-correction curve truncates the amplitude series after its first nonconstant term. Vary angle and length, comparing both the predicted period and the result a linear analysis would infer. Which change rescales both period curves together? Which changes their relative discrepancy?

Open the live experiment

Design a two-release check

For the same pendulum the ratio \(T(\theta_a)/T(\theta_b)=K(k_a)/K(k_b)\) cancels the unknown length and local gravity. Measure at two amplitudes before deciding whether to recalibrate the length. If the ratio agrees but both absolute times differ from prediction, check the effective length, clock rate and model of the bob. If the ratio changes during a decaying swing, record initial and final amplitudes for the timed sequence. The period associated with a long sequence is an average over its slowly changing amplitude. Substituting the initial angle throughout would overcorrect the measurement.

Tutorial

At small amplitude, what release angle gives a fractional period bias of approximately one part in a thousand?

Work through the solution

Set \(\theta_0^2/16=0.001\). This gives \(0.1265\) rad, about 7.25 degrees. The gravity bias from an uncorrected measurement would be approximately two parts in a thousand. A tolerance on one quantity is not automatically the same tolerance on the other.

A photogate records successive interruptions every 1.02 seconds for the metre pendulum. A calculation gives nearly four times local gravity. Identify one possible error in the observable.

Work through the solution

The gate may have counted both directions and returned a half period. Doubling that interval gives about 2.04 s. Because the inferred acceleration is inversely proportional to the square of the period, the missing factor of two gives a factor of four.

Why does reducing the grid spacing in an integration of the linear equation fail to recover the finite-amplitude curve?

Work through the solution

Integration error vanishes as the step is refined, but the restoring torque in that equation is \(mgL\theta\). The true torque is \(mgL\sin\theta\). A converged numerical solution can therefore provide independent evidence of an inadequate physical approximation.

For the equation of motion, phase portrait and dissipation, see the MIT treatment of the simple pendulum.

Applied study

Reconstruct a field from samples and estimate how interpolation changes its integral.

What lies between two measurements?

A probe records temperatures at equally spaced positions along a rod. To estimate the mean temperature, first decide what profile those samples represent. For neighbouring points \(x_j,x_{j+1}\) at distance \(h\), write \(x=x_j+sh\) with \(0\leq s\leq1\). The straight line matching both readings is

\[(I_h f)(x)=(1-s)f(x_j)+sf(x_{j+1}).\]

The weights are nonnegative and sum to one. This reproduces every affine function, keeps interpolated values between their neighbours, and amplifies bounded sample errors by at most a factor of one. A finer display grid can draw this line more densely. Recovering new curvature requires new source information.

Use curvature to predict the sign and size of error

For twice continuously differentiable \(f\), the interpolation remainder on one panel is \(f(x)-I_h f(x)=\tfrac12 f''(\xi)(x-x_j)(x-x_{j+1})\) for some \(\xi\) on that panel. The product is nonpositive and reaches magnitude \(h^2/4\) at the midpoint. Thus the maximum error is bounded by \(h^2\max|f''|/8\). For positive curvature the chord lies above the curve.

Integrating the chord gives \(h[f(x_j)+f(x_{j+1})]/2\), the trapezoidal rule. Integrating the bound with the quadratic factor retained gives a panel integral error at most \(h^3\max|f''|/12\). On a fixed interval of length \(L\) there are \(L/h\) panels, so the composite error is bounded by \(Lh^2\max|f''|/12\). The pointwise and total error both decrease quadratically, for different reasons.

Integrate a curved temperature profile

Use the exact profile \(T(x)=300+20(x/L)^2\) K with \(L=1\) m. Four equal panels have \(h=0.25\) m. On the first panel the endpoints are 300 and 301.25 K. Their interpolated midpoint is 300.625 K, while the exact midpoint is 300.3125 K. The overestimate of 0.3125 K is the same on every panel because the curvature is constant.

The exact integral is \(300+20/3=306.6667\) K·m on this unit-length rod. Here \(T''=40\) K/m² and the trapezoidal overestimate equals \(Lh^2 T''/12=0.20833\) K·m. Dividing the integral by length gives mean temperature. To obtain thermal energy above a reference temperature, additional material properties and cross-sectional area are needed.

Problem class

Choose the sensor spacing

For the quadratic rod, select the minimum number of equal intervals so the midpoint error never exceeds 0.05 K. How many measurements are needed including both endpoints?

Show the calculation

With \(N\) intervals on a metre, the bound is \(40/(8N^2)=5/N^2\) K. Requiring \(5/N^2\leq0.05\) gives \(N\geq10\), hence 11 measurements. At constant curvature this bound is attained at the midpoints.

Infer an integral from two estimates

Four and eight panels give 306.875 and 306.71875 K·m. Assume \(Q_h=Q+c h^2\). Eliminate \(c\) and estimate \(Q\). Would adding sensors only to the display grid make this correction possible?

Show the calculation

Multiply \(Q_{h/2}=Q+c h^2/4\) by four and subtract \(Q_h\). This gives \(Q=(4Q_{h/2}-Q_h)/3=306.66667\) K·m. For the quadratic profile the error law is exact. Subdividing the same source chords on screen preserves their total area and supplies no second source approximation.

OneSimulation laboratory

Measure what interpolation does to a heat integral

Compare recorded OneSimulation runs on this page. To test your own inputs, download the Python model and run the model in an activated OneSimulation environment.

OneSimulation maps source samples to the original nodes and their midpoints. Integrating those segments gives the area under the interpolant. Keep the quadratic temperature law and double the number of source intervals. Then restore four intervals and replace the quadratic law with \(300+20x/L\). Download the Python experiment · OneSimulation point interpolation.

python approximation-and-quadrature.py
python approximation-and-quadrature.py --cells 8
python approximation-and-quadrature.py --law linear
Errors against the exact source function and its integral.

Quadratic, 40.3125306.875+0.208333
Quadratic, 80.078125306.71875+0.0520833
Linear, 403100

Halving the spacing divides both quadratic-profile errors by four. The linear-profile result tests exactness on the function space represented by the interpolant.

Problem sheet 2 · Recover an integral using two biased readings

Notation and assumptions

Read each defining equation with its assumptions and units, then follow the link to the argument that gives the equation its meaning.

Barycentric weight

\[w_j=\prod_{i\ne j}(x_j-x_i)^{-1}\]

Only relative weights enter the rational expression for the interpolating polynomial. At a node return the recorded value directly.

Derivation · Evaluating an interpolant without rebuilding its polynomial

Fourier alias

\[e^{2\pi i(k+n)j/n}=e^{2\pi ikj/n}\]

On n equally spaced periodic samples two distinct continuum frequencies can yield precisely the same observations. Interpolation cannot disambiguate them without more information.

Derivation · When a trapezoidal sum counts the wrong frequency perfectly

Interpolation condition

\[p(x_i)=f_i\]

The polynomial matches the tabulated values at distinct nodes. Small nodal discrepancies do not guarantee a small error between nodes.

Derivation · Polynomial interpolation and node placement

Monte Carlo standard error

\[\mathrm{SE}(\bar f)=\sigma_f/\sqrt{N}\]

Independent samples with finite variance give this ideal error scale. Importance weights and correlations can substantially alter the variance of the actual estimator.

Derivation · Where should a random quadrature spend its samples?

Quadrature degree

\[Q[x^j]=\int_a^b x^j\,dx\quad(0\le j\le d)\]

Exactness through degree d is an algebraic property. For a nonsmooth or poorly sampled integrand the observed rate can be much lower.

Derivation · Gaussian quadrature and exactness

Richardson extrapolation

\[I\approx I_{h/2}+(I_{h/2}-I_h)/(2^p-1)\]

This cancels an assumed leading error proportional to h to power p. Several refinements can test whether that asymptotic regime is present.

Derivation · Deriving Romberg integration and recognising a broken error model

Further reading

  • Use the Chebyshev examples to connect coefficient decay and smoothness. Compare a function with an interior corner to one with a nearby complex singularity. Lloyd N. Trefethen · Approximation Theory and Approximation Practice

  • Chapters 5 and 9 give complementary local and global approximations. Compare those constructions on identical samples. Driscoll and Braun · Fundamentals of Numerical Computation
